Strategy in one line. The hiring manager judges fit for the actual work — and her public profile (EU inventory-placement strategy across the crossdock network; readiness for 50+ FC launches; a Kaizen / Lean trainer; teams built from scratch — public-profile inference in "Read me first") says she will test whether the mechanisms transfer to her world. Open with S11 (a boarding process behind published gates, run from a weekly input/output review) and hold S12 (a committed roadmap re-sequenced on cost-to-serve evidence). v2 adds five distinct Flexport primaries — S11-A the Monday review as an analysis, S11-B launch readiness as standard work, S11-C retiring an acquired product's book, S12-A the Asia latency investigation, S16-A the data model under the review — so every Leadership Principle has a primary and a backup from one company. Lead every answer with the mechanism left behind, the priced trade-off, and the team that grew to own it. Systems are enablers; the process redesign and its business results are the story.
LP focus — my guess, not confirmed: Deliver Results · Hire & Develop / Earth's Best Employer · Dive Deep. Amazon does not disclose the assignment; the guesses are reasoned from the interviewer's title, the JD and her public profile in "Read me first" (public-profile inference, labeled as such) and should be held loosely — Think Big / Frugality and Ownership / Earn Trust remain live alternates carried by the twist maps.
Also expect: "tell me about yourself" (75-second version, once, here) and "why Amazon" (hiring managers usually ask it); one functional question on how I would approach the NACF process space.

Every new story is tagged NEW and carries five labeled facets (data analysis · solution design · math & data logic · implementation · probe questions), four Before / Fix / After / Dollars tables, a four-question behavioral pack, three angle cards and three trap drills. Each primary opens with a SAFE-SAY box (the sentence for its most attackable number and for "what is your source for that?"). Yellow VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH tags mark only the specifics that are in none of the source files; each is repeated on the VERIFY page with the safe sentence to say instead.

5-Minute Cram Sheet — Snigdha Tripathy · Wed Oct 29 · 10:00 am PDT NEW

Her hooks → my proof (public-profile inference)
  • EU IXD placement strategy → S11 wave machine: a selection bar published before the first node, a ~50-container calibration per node, a public hold when a node misses; S11-C: three brokers = 75.0% of containers, so the few lanes that carry the volume get bespoke handling.
  • $80MM savings (her number — framing only) → my discipline on dollars: ~$5M avoidance said once (S12); $0.66 → $0.03; anything priced is "priced, not saved" (S16-A).
  • 50+ FC launches → S11-B readiness: four gates, 100 containers × 7 checks, six graduation criteria, and the pilot that ran later than the first testing plan, owned.
  • Kaizen / Lean trainer → S11-A waste = 3.67 touches and standard work; S11-B jidoka (stop the line, hold wave 3); S12-A genchi genbutsu and PDCA; S16-A poka-yoke (bridge table, parity gate).
Three opening sentences
  • Most significant delivery. "Cost-to-serve on Flexport's shipment-visibility product went from sixty-six cents a container to three while customers went from seven to sixty-eight — nine point seven times — with zero rollbacks, because boarding went through six published gates, not around them."
  • Changed a committed plan. "I re-opened my manager's eighteen-month roadmap privately, with numbers — partners first, operators on the same chassis — and value landed in month six with sixty-six percent less effort."
  • Dive into data. "A twenty-two-fold cost drop decomposes into two levers — containers needing a human, and touches on each — and which one moved decides what you do next."
Five numbers I must not get wrong
  • 7 → 68 = 9.7× — never rounded up; no other growth multiple is ever said.
  • $0.66 → $0.03, about 95%; direct dollars ≈ $50K a year modeled; the ~$1.5M pre-production estimate is a user anchor, separate, never added.
  • ~$5M is avoidance, said once — never annual; run-rate ≈ $1.5M a year is a separate modeled number.
  • S11-C: printed $185,892 = six of nine segments; nine rows = $254,952; lead with containers (294,581).
  • S12-A: never quote 81%; like-for-like is −44.2% overall (Apr → Sep). S16-A: $103,680 and $100,360 are prices, not savings.

Bridge in one breath (booklet frameworks)
  • Inbound capacity: dock doors × hours × unload rate; utilization knee ≈ 85%; labor by UPH; cubic fill.
  • Inventory health: weeks of cover — under 2 expedite, 2–4 healthy, over 6 excess; safety stock z · σ · √LT.
  • Placement: distance-to-customer priced against cost per unit moved — the edge-near-demand logic of S12-A, the concentration logic of S11-C.
Room rules
  • Pitch, land the metric, stop. Name who owned what — program-lead voice.
  • Every dollar with its basis word; if a number is yellow in the text, check the last page first.
  • Overlaps: S16-A, S11-C, S12-A, S11-B facts are also in other packets — see "Read me first"; fall back to the router's backup if the panel flags a clash.
  • Bridge in one sentence, then hand it back to her with a question.
  • If challenged on a number, read the story's SAFE-SAY box; if it is yellow, read its safe sentence on the VERIFY page — never the unconfirmed specific.

B-cards: each story's Behavioral Pack has four separate 90-second STARs (B1–B4), each headed by the real AIQB question it answers; the three angle cards (data · conflict · honest failure) give a different opener and metric when the same story is asked twice.

Read me first

The interviewer's lens (inference from title, JD and public profile — the profile parts labeled)

Inference from the title. The hiring manager owns the team this role joins and the outcomes it is measured on. A Senior Manager of Supply Chain in NACF is accountable for network-level processes — how inventory is planned, placed, moved and recovered across fulfillment centers — and for the operating cadence (weekly business reviews, goals, mechanisms) that keeps those processes honest. She is the one interviewer who will also judge fit for the actual work: can this person define and run a process across teams that do not report to them, make value legible to leadership, and change a committed plan on evidence without breaking trust.

What that means for the answers. Lead with scale-with-mechanisms (S11) and roadmap judgment under unit economics (S12); v2 adds five distinct Flexport primaries (S11-A, S11-B, S11-C, S12-A, S16-A) so every Leadership Principle has a primary and a backup from one company — see the Router. All seven are Flexport stories, told as process redesigns: a boarding process behind published gates with a weekly input/output review, a launch roadmap re-sequenced on cost-to-serve, and the analysis, readiness, retirement, latency and data-model work underneath them. She will probably also ask the openers — "tell me about yourself" and "why Amazon" — and a functional question on how I would approach the NACF process space in the first 90 days. The JD's own phrase, "designing or modeling the supply chain as opposed to working in it," is the lens to speak to: I designed the boarding process and the review; I did not run the ops desk.

What her public profile adds (public-profile inference, labeled)

The profile, as pasted (public-profile inference — directional, not verified biography). 11+ years at Amazon across operations, launch and learning: EU IXD & Placement Operations Manager, Luxembourg, 2017–21 — defined the EU inventory-placement strategy for the crossdock network (distance-to-customer, speed-vs-cost) and built the team ground-up (+$50MM savings in 2020); Senior Manager EU IXD, 2021–22 — ran the EU crossdock network with a team of six plus interns ($80MM savings in 2021; IXD grown to 60% of inbound, +46% YoY); Senior Launch Program Manager, 2014–17 — readiness for 50+ FC launches including Amazon.in; FC Learning & Development Manager — Kaizen / Lean / CI training, learning curves and skill matrices, delivered an S-Team goal; Dell training background; SP Jain PGPM. These are her numbers from her profile, quoted for framing only — they are not canon and never enter my stories.

What that means for the answers. Three adjustments. First, lead with the mechanism left behind and who owns it now — she built teams and mechanisms herself (a placement strategy, a crossdock network, an L&D program), so "what still runs without you?" is her native question. Second, price the trade-off the way she priced distance-to-customer against speed and cost — S11's boarding pace vs automation maturity and S12's operators' two further quarters are exactly her shape of decision. Third, expect transferability probes — "how would this apply to a placement / network problem?" — and expect her to spot borrowed Leadership-Principle language: answer in the story's own words (gates, denominators, reversal triggers), never in recited LP slogans.

Answer strategy — business first, systems as enablers

  • Open with the process problem and its cost, not the system. First sentence names the broken process (Plan, Source, Make, Deliver or Return), who it hurt (customer, Finance, the floor) and the baseline number. The platform appears only as the enabler of the redesign, in one clause.
  • Use the JD's four outcome words as the results frame: reliability (did the promise hold?), responsiveness (how much earlier did we see it?), cost efficiency ($ with its basis label), asset utilization (capital, labor-hours, capacity released).
  • Action = operating-model redesign in gated phases — Discovery → Plan & Design → Build & Test → Cutover → Hypercare, each spoken as entry bar · what I delivered · exit bar. Say the gate that fired, not the meeting that happened.
  • Every dollar carries its basis word in the same breath — record · user anchor · modeled · priced. A record dollar is said flat; a modeled dollar is said with its inputs. Never promote a modeled number to record.
  • Program-lead voice. I owned definitions, rules, gates and the signature chain; engineers, SIs and vendor teams configured and built to the spec I signed. The only platform personally configured in canon is Manhattan DOM.
  • The pitch ends on the primary metric. Then stop. Silence after the pitch is the interviewer filling their STAR worksheet; their probes are the second half of the answer.
  • For the HM specifically: name the mechanism in Amazon's own vocabulary where it is honest — a weekly input/output review with frozen metric definitions, graduation criteria published before the wave, a reversal trigger written before the flip. Do not claim to have invented the WBR; say it was copied from a trusted review, which is the point.
  • Answer her native question — what did you leave behind, and who owns it now? Every story lands on a mechanism that still runs without me (the Monday review and the graduation gates; the rebuild-vs-reuse rule and the scope tripwire) with a named owner. She built teams and mechanisms ground-up (public-profile inference); that is the evidence she pattern-matches.
  • Price the trade-off in her units. She priced distance-to-customer against speed and cost for a crossdock network (public-profile inference); my versions are boarding pace vs automation maturity (S11) and partners-first vs operators' two further quarters, priced and published (S12). Say the trade and its price, not just the win.
  • Two warnings (public-profile inferences). She tests transferability — "how would this apply to a placement / network problem?" — so practice the bridges in the twist maps before the call. And she spots borrowed LP language: never recite slogans ("I was customer-obsessed"); say the gate, the denominator, the reversal trigger in the story's own words.
  • Have the openers ready — the 75-second "tell me about yourself" and the 60-second "why Amazon" from the master packet (newest to oldest; Sephora → Flexport → Elementum → Manhattan). Say the long version once, here.
Loop-consistency rule. One question, one story; never the same story to two interviewers — interviewers compare notes. Held for other packets (do not spend here): S1/S3 (Lan) · S2/S18 (Goforth) · S6/S8 (Hoerger) · S15/S14 (Josyula). v2 overlap flags — confirm before the loop. This packet deliberately tells five Flexport records that another packet also touches: S16-A (Snowflake data model; Josyula's primary is S16, told as Ownership and the security-review miss — here lead with grain, join and reconciliation; the S16-A failure card is that same two-week miss, so if the panel notes show Josyula tells it, give it one sentence and pivot to the data model); S11-C (the Crux customer transition — the loop packet's S10, "79 customers, a destination and a date each"; no other panel carries it as a primary, but Goforth's pilot bench touches the Crux shadow accounts); S12-A (Asia latency; Hoerger's doc bench); S11-B (pilot rollout schedule and testing approach; Goforth's doc bench); the audit-logs PRD and the schedules solution are Lan's bench and are not used here. If the panel notes show a clash, drop the flagged story to a one-line pointer and use its router backup. If pushed for "another example" on an LP, use the named backup in the router, the question bank or the backup bench, never a story allocated to another interviewer.
How to read the "L6 level evidence" block under each story. Amazon's L6 functional bar (user-supplied rubric): Ambiguity — strategy or network undefined; I select the stakeholders, set the goals, deliver independently. Scope & influence — team-level scope; influences one to two levels up. Execution — tactical and strategic; simplifies or extends; applies best practices; force-multiplies; mitigates long-term risk; makes the short-versus-long-term trade-off explicit. Impact — long-term team goals. Process improvement — operational-excellence best practices institutionalized. Complex — visible risks, roadblocks and constraints mitigated through expertise, judgment and influence to consensus. Overview themes the block is tuned to: designing and modeling the supply chain rather than working in it; developing new processes for others to own; root-cause mechanisms that resolve trending issues at scale; recommendations to leadership that outline competing metrics. The block sits right after Results so the evidence is on the page when the probing starts.

The stories — business-first, canon numbers

Seven Flexport primaries: two flagships (S11, S12 — deepened, with the original pages kept) and five new (S11-A, S11-B, S11-C, S12-A, S16-A). Each: 60-second pitch → SAFE-SAY box → Situation/Task → Action in gated phases → Results → L6 evidence → five labeled facets (data analysis · solution design · math & data logic · implementation · probe questions) → four-table math → sensitivity → statements → 20-second version and NACF bridge → behavioral pack → three angles → three trap drills.

S11 — Flexport — Shipment Visibility launch: a boarding process behind published graduation gates, run from a weekly input/output review (cost-to-serve $0.66 → $0.03 across 7 → 68 customers) DEEPENED

LPs this story can serve here: Deliver Results (primary) · Ownership (accepted the critique, owned the measurement) · Customer Obsession (graduation criteria per wave, CSAT in the gate) · Earn Trust (the discarded 99.4% week) · Hire & Develop (backup — wave teams grown into owners)
Process family: Deliver — customer onboarding / service-delivery process; weekly operating cadence; launch readiness and staged rollout
SAFE-SAY — two sentences to say exactly

Most attackable number. “Seven to sixty-eight customers — nine point seven times — and a hundred and seventy monthly users, both landed two months early; thirty-two thousand shipments was the target I managed toward, not a result I claim.”

“What's your source for that?” “The targets and the roll-out record are in the program's own documents and my interview workbook; nine point seven is my division, sixty-eight over seven, and the cost-to-serve arc of about sixty-six cents to about three cents is the same record, rounded to about ninety-five percent.”

Leadership Principles this story carries — one-line proof sentence each

Deliver Results Both public targets — 68 customers and 170 monthly active users — landed two months early with zero rollbacks (the 32,000-shipment figure was a target I managed toward, not a result), because boarding went through six published graduation criteria rather than around them.

Ownership I accepted the critique (#3 to #14 of 16) as being about evidence, not effort, and owned the measurement system myself — by hand every Monday for months — before it was handed to a pipeline.

Customer Obsession CSAT at or above the consignee average was inside the gate, every client entered on a ~50-container calibration of its own freight, and 100 real containers ran seven checks before the first paying customer.

Earn Trust A flattering 99.4% week resting on three touches was printed as a data miss, and wave 3 was held in public on an ETA-accuracy miss — trust came from numbers that survived the next Monday.

Honesty label. Program owner across nine teams / ~100 people; the WBR format was copied from an existing leadership review (the LSA review), not invented; build-week counts are illustrative; the ~$1.5M pre-production measurement saving is a separate user-anchor figure (my own estimate, not a program-document number), never merged with cost-to-serve. Nine teams built and operated; I owned the boarding process, the measurement system and the pre-prod proof.
What is Shipment Visibility? — the one-minute explainer (from the program documents; record-from-docs)

The minute. Shipment Visibility — "SV" — is Flexport's client-facing visibility product in the Client App: one pane where a client watches container milestones across their whole book, Flexport-carried and non-Flexport-carried shipments alike, so track-and-trace stops being emails to an ops desk and becomes a product the client logs into. It also created a "visibility-only" client segment — companies that bought visibility first were a deliberate upsell path into Flexport freight. The 2021 plan priced the problem it solved: roughly $3.92M a year of manual track-and-trace salary across the ops org against about $263K of vendor spend, with only 18 of 99 milestones automated at the time. record-from-docs

The machinery behind the story. Product standards: coverage 90%+, accuracy 95%, timeliness 90%, a fail-safe switch, dashboards and alarms — under the operating rule "edits are the enemy": every human correction is an automation defect, not a fix. Rollout: a free pilot in graduated waves — wave 1 two named clients (the first two hand-carried customers) around Q4 2021, wave 2 four of a seven-client pool around Q1 2022, wave 3 twenty-plus around Q2 2022, full roll-out H2 2022 — each client entering on a ~50-container calibration and graduating on six published criteria. Testing: four gates before launch — a 100-container live test, biweekly bug bashes on the client job stories, shadow accounts fed real Crux data, and UAT gated on data quality (80% of shipments minor-to-no issues). And Crux — the acquired visibility product — was transitioned onto SV by end 2022: 79 customers, $185,892 printed ARR (six of nine segments — S11-C re-foots it), 294,581 containers. record-from-docs

Doc-vs-canon footnotes: the pilot schedule phrases the SLA criterion as "90% of verifications within 24–48h"; canon says "reviewed within 24h" — canon wins, say 24h. The 95% accuracy standard is the 2021 plan's bar for milestone data, not this story's ETA-accuracy metric (60% → 90%, canon) — never mix them. The $3.92M / $263K figures are the plan's ops-org business case, a different basis from canon's per-container CTS — never merged. Wave timing and the six criteria match the master packet.

60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Flexport's shipment-visibility product had public commitments — 68 monthly active customers, 170 MAU, 32K shipments — and was serving seven pilots at a cost-to-serve of $0.66 a container, most of it human touches. Sales wanted the entire pool boarded at once; leadership said the program was not demonstrating value and it was about to drop from #3 to #14 of 16 initiatives. The process problem was that boarding ran faster than the automation could carry, and nobody could see it. Task. As program owner across nine teams, I owned the boarding path, the measurement system and the pre-production proof. Action. I rebuilt boarding as a gated process: six graduation criteria published to Sales before wave one, a launch gate of 100 real containers across seven checks, and a weekly input/output review — seven frozen metric definitions, each signed by the owner who would be red against it, every input tagged to the output it moves — copied from a review leadership already trusted and run by hand every Monday for months. Waves boarded only through the criteria; wave three was held in public on an ETA-accuracy miss and became the cleanest wave. When a flattering 99.4% week turned out to rest on three touches, I printed a data miss instead. Result. Primary metric: cost-to-serve per container $0.66 → $0.03, about 95%, across customers 7 → 68 — 9.7× — with zero rollbacks and no added ops headcount; automation 40% → 90%, accuracy 60% → 90%, both public targets two months early. The ~$1.5M pre-production measurement saving is a separate user-anchor figure — my own estimate — and I keep it separate.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — Senior leadership told me the visibility program was not demonstrating value at the anticipated rate — it would drop from #3 to #14 of 16 initiatives. I had been reporting activity, not evidence. The critique was about evidence, not effort, and I accepted that first.

The business process underneath: customer boarding (Deliver) ran as a sales push — board the whole pool now — against an operation where most containers were still touched by hand. Cost-to-serve was $0.66 a container (≈3.7 human interactions at the record cross-walk of $0.18 per interaction); automation covered 40% of containers; ETA accuracy was 60%. The public commitments were 68 monthly active customers, 170 MAU and 32K shipments, off seven pilots.

The cost of leaving it: a bad first impression at scale with rollbacks, ops headcount growing with the customer count, and the program losing its funding slot because value was invisible.

T — Program owner across nine teams / ~100 people: own the boarding path, the measurement system and the pre-production proof — and make value legible to the leadership that decides rollout pace and funding, with zero rollbacks and no added ops headcount.

Baselines on the table: customers 7 (target 68) · cost-to-serve $0.66/container · automation 40% · ETA accuracy 60% · public targets 68 customers / 170 MAU / 32K shipments · no instrument leadership trusted.

Key challenges — what the program record adds (from the program documents; record-from-docs)
  • Rollout sequencing under a free pilot. Pilot clients pay nothing, so every wave spent engineering, data and ops capacity before revenue; the pool had to be sequenced 2 → 4-of-7 → 20+ against a ~50-container calibration per client and six graduation criteria, with GO/NO-GO decisions taken monthly. That is the doc-level version of "board no faster than the automation can carry." record-from-docs
  • Testing gates before any launch. Four gates stood between build and client: a 100-container live test watched end-to-end, biweekly bug bashes on the client job stories, shadow accounts (real Crux data in dedicated demo accounts), and UAT that did not start until 80% of shipments showed minor-to-no issues. The gates are why "zero rollbacks" was achievable. record-from-docs
  • An acquired product's customers in the middle of the rollout. Crux's 79 customers — $185,892 printed ARR (six of nine segments — see S11-C), 294,581 containers — had to be transitioned onto SV by end 2022, including cancel-vs-transition decisions by segment and per-customer calls for the largest accounts, while the pilot waves were still boarding. Migration risk ran in parallel with launch risk. record-from-docs
  • Data quality as a product standard, not an ops task. The 2021 plan set coverage 90%+ / accuracy 95% / timeliness 90% with a fail-safe switch, and named the enemy: human edits. "Edits are the enemy" is the sentence behind the story's automation 40% → 90%. record-from-docs
Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery — wrote the honest diagnosis (activity, not evidence); inventoried what leadership actually decides on (rollout pace, funding); chose to copy the LSA review's format rather than invent one. Exit bar: leadership agrees the questions the review must answer weekly; ~50-container calibration entry bar per client.
  • Plan & Design — froze seven metric definitions (coverage auto, coverage with human, tickets, tickets per active container, 4PL SLA %, 3PL SLA %, delta); input→output tagging; the ±5% week-over-week red/green rule; the 15-day active rule as the honest denominator; six graduation criteria published to Sales before wave 1 — DQ ≥ 90%, ETA ≥ 80%, SLA 90% reviewed within 24h, ≥100 containers + 5 features, stability, CSAT ≥ consignee average; launch gate 100 real containers × 7 checks; pre-prod sample size 300 containers for ±1.6% at 95% confidence. Exit bar: each input owner signs the definition they will be red against; first Monday deck dry-run.
  • Build & Test — ran the review by hand every Monday (1.5–2 hrs, later ~4); pre-decomposed every red with an owner and a dated fix; enforced the data-honesty rule — a truncated week is a data miss, not a result; pre-prod ingest with per-hop monitors and kill switches; load proof 500 containers / 20 min / 99.6%. Exit bar: two consecutive Mondays where every red arrives with an owner and the number verifies the following week.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (waves) — W1 (2 clients) → W2 (4 of a 7-pool) → W3 (20+) held in public on an ETA-accuracy miss traced to one bad source → full rollout. The Monday review became the forum where the rollout decision was taken. Exit bar: the VP unlocks the 68-client rollout two months early off the review's own numbers.
  • Hypercare / Close — discarded a flattering 99.4% three-touch week and printed a data miss; handed the manual pull to a pipeline with the definitions in code (S16-A in this packet tells the data model; the full S16 telling belongs to Josyula's packet); left the critic steering by the numbers. Exit bar: automation 40 → 90%; CTS $0.66 → $0.03; same ops team at 68 customers.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the diagnosis, the frozen definitions, the review built by hand weekly, the graduation criteria, the hold decisions and the pre-prod measurement design. Nine teams built and operated; each input owner signed the definition they would be red against; the VP took the rollout decisions in the review. The Sales push to board everything now was answered with gates published before wave 1 — so when wave 3 was held, it was the rule firing, not a judgment call; the held customers became the cleanest wave.

Designed the process; others own it now. I designed the review, the definitions and the graduation gates; the input owners across the nine teams run their lines and the pipeline (S16-A) carries the definitions in code — the review steers without me. Trade-off put to leadership. My recommendation to leadership outlined the competing metrics explicitly — boarding pace and customer count vs automation coverage and ETA accuracy — and proposed to board no faster than the automation could carry; wave 3 was the trade made in public.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality (canon numbers only)
  • $ — cost-to-serve per container $0.66 → $0.03 (record; about 95%); direct CTS dollars ≈$50K/yr modeled on an illustrative container count — the value is scale without headcount, not the direct dollars; the ~$1.5M pre-production measurement saving is a separate user-anchor figure, never merged with CTS.
  • # — customers 7 → 68 (9.7×) with zero rollbacks and no added ops headcount; targets 68 customers / 170 MAU / 32K shipments (record).
  • % — CTS about 95%; automation 40% → 90%; ETA accuracy 60% → 90% (record).
  • Time — both public targets hit two months early; the rollout unlocked two months early off the review (record); pre-prod measurement path cut rollout proof from 6 months to 1.5.
  • Customer — six graduation criteria published to Sales before wave 1 — including CSAT ≥ consignee average; wave 3 held in public on an ETA-accuracy miss and became the cleanest wave (record).
  • Quality — a flattering truncated 99.4% week discarded and printed as a data miss; seven metric definitions frozen and later moved into code (record).
L6 level evidence — what the interviewer pattern-matches against the Amazon L6 functional rubric; every line is this story's canon, no new numbers

Probing — challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer

HM — Why is this your Deliver Results story and not just a metrics story?

Because the results landed behind gates, not around them: both public targets two months early, 7 → 68 customers on the same ops team, zero rollbacks. The review and the graduation criteria are the mechanism; the about-95% cut and the 9.7× are the outcome.

HM — The direct dollars on a 95% cost reduction are small. Why lead with it?

Because it is honest and because the point was never the direct dollars: at the 32K-shipment target the direct saving is ≈$50K a year, modeled on an illustrative container count. What it bought is boarding 9.7× the customers without 9.7× the ops headcount — roughly two and a half people never hired (modeled — assumptions in the deepening's dollars table; not record headcount) — and a rollout unlocked two months early. The ~$1.5M pre-prod estimate is a separate user anchor and I never add it to this.

HM — Sales wanted everyone boarded. How did you hold the line without becoming the blocker?

By publishing the six criteria to Sales before wave one, so the rule did the refusing. Wave 3 was held in public on an ETA-accuracy miss traced to one bad source; the customers we held became the cleanest wave, and Sales had seen the gate before it fired.

HM — You discarded a 99.4% week. Walk me through that decision.

Three touches is not a week — a 95% confidence interval on N = 3 is wider than the metric. Printing it would have taught the VP a number that could not survive the next Monday. I printed a data miss. A review that flatters once is not trusted twice.

HM — What would you do differently, and how does this translate to my team?

Start the review at kickoff, not after the critique. At Amazon this is a WBR story told to the company that invented the WBR — the respectful version is that I copied a trusted format, froze definitions with their owners, tagged inputs to outputs and let the review make the rollout decision. Graduation criteria are launch gates published before the wave and held in public.

HM — What was yours and what was the team's?

Mine: the diagnosis, the seven definitions, the Monday build by hand for months, the criteria, the holds, the pre-prod measurement design. The nine teams built and operated; each input owner signed their own definition; the VP decided in the room. The format came from the LSA review — I did not invent it.

HM — Why waves at all? What did the calibration actually buy?

The pilot was free, so every boarded client cost engineering, data and ops before revenue — boarding the whole pool would have spent the program's credibility on clients we could not yet serve. The ~50-container calibration per client gave the data team a verified baseline on the client's own freight before scale, and the six published criteria converted "ready" from a feeling into a rule: wave 1 two clients, wave 2 four of a seven-client pool, wave 3 twenty-plus record-from-docs. The canon version: waves boarded only through the published gates, and the held wave became the cleanest.

HM — How did you test before the first paying customer touched it?

Four gates, all from the program record record-from-docs: a 100-container live test watched end-to-end; biweekly bug bashes on the client job stories; shadow client accounts — real Crux data fed into dedicated demo accounts so the clients' own data exposed the defects; and UAT that did not start until 80% of shipments showed minor-to-no issues. UAT was gated on data quality, not on the calendar — the testing version of the graduation rule.

HM — You were rolling out while an acquired product's customers sat on the old platform. How did you handle that risk?

As a parallel migration with its own decision rules, not an afterthought record-from-docs: 79 Crux customers, $185,892 printed ARR (six of nine segments — see S11-C) and 294,581 containers, segmented — transition where SV fit, cancel honestly where it did not (two competitor SCM-SaaS accounts were canceled on published dates), per-customer calls for the largest accounts. Internal pushback on migration cost and feature parity was priced into the decision document, not overridden. The pilot gates kept quality honest while the migration ran beside it.

The key math — cost-to-serve, the scale result, and why the dollars stay separate
StepQuantityFormula → substitutionBasisValue
1Cost-to-serve before$0.66 / $0.18 ≈ 3.7 touches per containerrecord; $0.18 per interaction is the record cross-walk$0.66 — ≈3.7 touches
2Cost-to-serve after($0.03 − $0.66) / $0.66 = −95.45%, say "about 95%"; $0.03 / $0.18 = 0.17 touchesrecord$0.03 — ≈0.17 touches
3Scale68 / 7 = 9.7×; zero rollbacks; no added ops headcountrecord9.7× (never "10×")
4Direct CTS dollars32,000 shipments × 1.5–4 containers = 48–128K; × $0.63 = $30–81Kmodeled (containers per shipment illustrative)≈$50K/yr modeled
5Pre-prod measurement pathn = 300 → ±1.6% at 95% CI; rollout proof 1.5 vs 6 monthsuser anchor — separate canonical~$1.5M, never merged with CTS
6Week-over-week ruleWoW = (B − A)/A = (21.9 − 40.0)/40.0 = −45% → red±5% red/green (record design; values are the 17 Jan and 31 Jan 2022 prints)root cause: a carrier format change — measurement, not operators

Automation 40 → 90% and the WBR hours (1.5–2 → ~4 by hand) are this packet's canon; the loop packet carries different values (§1.3 conflict) — do not mix them in the room.

Where this lands at Amazon. This is the JD's "define, create, implement and execute processes" line in its purest form: a boarding process with published graduation criteria, a weekly input/output review with frozen definitions, and the discipline to hold a wave in public. Reliability (zero rollbacks), responsiveness (two months early), cost efficiency (about 95% CTS), asset utilization (the same ops team at 9.7× the customers).

Controls. Nine input owners each sign the definition they will be red against; reds are pre-decomposed with an owner and a dated fix; per-hop monitors and kill switches on the pre-production ingest; the manual pull is the review's source until the pipeline matches within 1% (S16-A). Lean / Kaizen mapping. The six criteria are standard work for onboarding; the hold on wave 3 is jidoka — stop the line when a quality criterion fails; the Monday review is the PDCA Check; "edits are the enemy" is the 2021 plan's own waste statement — every human correction is a defect, not a fix.

Facet 5 — Probe questions (numeric)

Why is the direct dollar figure so small?

Because the unit economics is the point: $0.63 saved on 48,000–128,000 containers is $30,240–$80,640, modeled on an illustrative containers-per-shipment. What it bought was 68 customers on the same ops team — about 2.5 people never hired, modeled — and a rollout unlocked two months early.

Prove the 2.5 people.

It is a model with two assumptions I name: 80,000 containers × 3.5 touches removed × one minute = 4,667 hours; at 1,800 hours a year that is 2.6 FTE, at 1,920 it is 2.4. I would replace the minute with the ticket-grain automation data.

Why ±1.6%?

That is my sample design, a user anchor — the program's own evidence was 500 containers in 20 minutes at 99.6% and a dry run at 100% processed, 86% tracked. n = 300 at about 98% accuracy: 1.96 × √(0.98 × 0.02 / 300) = 0.0158. Big enough to certify the gate, small enough to afford — it cut the rollout proof from 6 months to 1.5, which is the separate ~$1.5M pre-production estimate (user anchor), never merged with cost-to-serve.

Is it 10× or 9.7×?

68 / 7 = 9.71. Say 7 to 68, or 9.7 times; no other multiple belongs in the room.

What does −45% week-over-week mean?

(21.9 − 40.0) / 40.0 = −45%, red against ±5%. The weekly prints were 40.0% on 17 January and 21.9% on 31 January 2022 (record); the root cause was a carrier format change — measurement, not operators.

How do you know a wave is ready?

Six criteria all pass — data quality at least 90%, ETA accuracy at least 80%, SLA 90% reviewed within 24 hours, at least 100 containers and 5 features, stability, CSAT at or above the consignee average — after a ~50-container calibration.

What would you change?

Start the review at kickoff instead of after the critique, and price the pipeline hand-off earlier — the pull stayed with me for months.

Sensitivity — containers per shipment (the soft input)

Total impact statement — say it like this

Honestly, the cost-to-serve number is a unit-economics result, not a big-dollar number: ninety-five percent off per container is measured, and at the thirty-two-thousand-shipment target it is only about fifty thousand dollars a year of direct cost — my model, on an illustrative container count. What it bought is scale: sixty-eight customers on the same ops team with zero rollbacks, about two and a half people we never had to hire — modeled — and a rollout unlocked two months early. The million and a half on the pre-production measurement path is a separate figure — my own estimate — and I keep it separate. So: the ninety-five percent and the seven-to-sixty-eight are record; every dollar except that one-and-a-half is my model.

If challenged — the full answers

"Your containers-per-shipment is a guess."

It is, and I said so: 32,000 shipments is a target, not a count, and it is shipments, not containers. At 1.5 to 4 containers per shipment the direct saving runs $30,240 to $80,640. I would pull the active-container count from the 15-day active rule the review already uses.

"Fifty thousand a year is small — why lead with it?"

Because it is honest, and the point was never the direct dollars: it was boarding 9.7 times the customers without 9.7 times the ops headcount.

"Where does the million and a half come from, then?"

The pre-production measurement path: 300 containers at ±1.6% proved the product in 1.5 months instead of 6. It is my estimate (a user anchor, not a program-document number), it is not cost-to-serve, and I never add it to the fifty thousand.

20-second version and NACF bridge
20-second version

At Flexport I owned the boarding process for Shipment Visibility: six published graduation criteria and a Monday input-output review took cost-to-serve from $0.66 to $0.03 per container while customers went from 7 to 68, with zero rollbacks and 68 customers and 170 monthly users landed two months early.

NACF bridge — one sentence

It is the shape of a network launch: onboard each node behind criteria published before the first one, calibrate on a trial volume, and hold a node in public when it misses — scale follows the gate, never the ask.

Behavioral pack — four questions this story answers, each a separate 90-second STAR from a different facet

B1 · “Tell me about a time when you not only met a goal but considerably exceeded expectations. How were you able to do it? What challenges did you have to overcome?” AIQB: Deliver Results Q3 Deliver Results facet: implementation

Open (first two sentences): “Both public targets landed two months early with zero rollbacks — and the way they landed is the point: boarding went through six published criteria, not around them.”

90-second STAR. S Seven pilots against public targets of 68 customers and 170 monthly active users (plus a 32,000-shipment target); Sales wanted the whole pool boarded at once. T Hit the targets without a rollback or added ops headcount. A Waves — 2, then 4 of 7, then 20-plus — each entering on a ~50-container calibration and graduating on six criteria published to Sales before wave 1, with a launch gate of 100 real containers across seven checks and a Monday review taking the rollout decision. R Customers 7 → 68, cost-to-serve $0.66 → $0.03, automation 40% → 90%, accuracy 60% → 90%; zero rollbacks.

Land: 7 → 68 (9.7×); 68 customers and 170 users two months early  ·  Pivot to NACF: launching nodes in a network: published gates, calibrated volumes, a public hold when a node misses.

Do NOT say: "10×" or any other multiple; the $1.5M added to cost-to-serve.

B2 · “Give me an example of a tough or critical piece of feedback you received. What was it and what did you do about it?” AIQB: Earn Trust Q2 Ownership facet: data analysis

Open (first two sentences): “Senior leadership told me the program was not demonstrating value and would drop from third to fourteenth of sixteen. I had been reporting activity, not evidence — and I accepted that first.”

90-second STAR. S A program about to lose its funding slot because value was invisible. T Make value legible without arguing the critique. A I inventoried what leadership actually decides on, copied a review format they already trusted, froze seven metrics with owners who signed the definitions they would be red against, and ran the pull by hand every Monday for months. R The same critic steered the rollout by the review's numbers, and unlocked the 68-client rollout two months early.

Land: #3 → #14 of 16 → the VP unlocked the rollout  ·  Pivot to NACF: owning a metric nobody owned: build it by hand first, automate it once the definitions are signed.

Do NOT say: that I invented the weekly review — I copied the LSA format.

B3 · “Tell me about a time when you had to balance the needs of the customer with the needs of the business. What did you do? What was the result?” AIQB: Customer Obsession Q8 Customer Obsession facet: solution design

Open (first two sentences): “Wave three was held in public because ETA accuracy missed on one bad source. Sales wanted speed; the customers we held became the cleanest wave.”

90-second STAR. S A free pilot where every boarded client cost engineering, data and ops before revenue. T Protect each client's first impression at scale. A Six criteria published before wave 1 — including CSAT at or above the consignee average — a ~50-container calibration on each client's own freight, and a hold on wave 3 traced to one bad source. R Zero rollbacks; the held wave became the cleanest.

Land: zero rollbacks; wave 3 held  ·  Pivot to NACF: inbound readiness: do not take the first container until the node's criteria pass.

Do NOT say: that Sales was wrong — they were right to want speed; the gate did the refusing.

B4 · “Tell me about a time when you uncovered a significant problem in your team. What was it and how did you communicate it to your manager and other stakeholders? What did you do to address the problem?” AIQB: Earn Trust Q6 Earn Trust facet: math and data logic

Open (first two sentences): “One week showed ninety-nine point four percent — on three touches. A 95% confidence interval on three is wider than the metric, so I printed a data miss instead.”

90-second STAR. S A flattering week landing just before a funding decision. T Keep the review credible. A I computed the interval — Wilson lower bound 0.44 on N = 3 — and held the review to the 15-day active denominator; the week was printed as a data miss. R A number that survived the next Monday; the review was trusted twice.

Land: 99.4% on N = 3 → data miss  ·  Pivot to NACF: any capacity report: a rate on a tiny denominator is not a result.

Do NOT say: the Wilson math unless asked — say "three touches is not a week".

Same story, three angles — three cards, three openers, three metrics, three LPs

Data / analytical angle Dive Deep

Opener: “Cost-to-serve was sixty-six cents a container — which is three and two-thirds human touches. The program was a touches problem, not a headcount problem.”

Headline metric: $0.66 / $0.18 = 3.67 → 0.17 touches

  • Decompose dollars into touches with the record cross-walk.
  • Active denominator: cumulative inflow less containers 15 days past departed terminal.
  • WoW rule: −45% is red; root cause a carrier format change.
  • Say "modeled" on every dollar.

Conflict / influence / backbone angle Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit

Opener: “Sales wanted the entire pool boarded at once; I published the gates to Sales before wave one, so when wave three was held, it was the rule firing — not me.”

Headline metric: six criteria; wave 3 held

  • The rule did the refusing; no one was called wrong.
  • The held customers became the cleanest wave.
  • The trade: boarding speed for a rollout that never rolled back.
  • Leadership took the decision in the Monday review.

Honest failure-and-learning angle Learn and Be Curious

Opener: “I had been reporting activity, not evidence — leadership said so, and they were right. The review should have started at kickoff, not after the critique.”

Headline metric: #3 → #14 of 16

  • What I got wrong: measuring effort instead of outcomes.
  • What I changed: seven frozen definitions, input → output tagging.
  • What I would do differently: kickoff review; earlier pipeline hand-off.
  • What held: zero rollbacks.
Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups

"Customers 7 to 68 — that's a 10× result?"

Numeric answer. 68 / 7 = 9.71. The only multiple to say is 9.7× — or just 7 to 68. Rounding up to 10× overstates a record number by 3%.

Say exactly: “Seven to sixty-eight — nine point seven times, and I won't round that up.”

"You said two and a half people never hired. Show me."

Numeric answer. 80,000 containers × 3.5 touches removed × 1 minute = 4,667 hours; ÷ 1,800 h = 2.6 FTE (÷ 1,920 h = 2.4). Modeled: containers per shipment, one minute per touch and the hours per FTE are my assumptions; the record is zero added ops headcount at 68 customers.

Say exactly: “It's modeled: about two and a half people on an illustrative container count and a minute a touch — the record is that headcount didn't grow.”

"So the saving is $1.5M plus $50K?"

Numeric answer. No. The ~$1.5M is the pre-production measurement saving (n = 300 → ±1.6%, 1.5 vs 6 months) — my estimate and a user anchor, kept separate. The $50,400 is modeled direct cost-to-serve at 80,000 containers. They never add.

Say exactly: “They're separate. The million and a half is my estimate of the pre-production proof path, kept separate; the fifty thousand is my model of direct cost-to-serve.”

S11-A — Flexport — The Monday review as an analysis: taking a 22× cost-to-serve drop apart (6.0× coverage × 3.67× touch efficiency), with honest denominators, seven frozen metrics and one discarded week NEW

LPs this story can serve here: Dive Deep (primary) · Insist on the Highest Standards · Earn Trust · Are Right, A Lot (backup)
Process family: Plan / Deliver — operating cadence, measurement design and cost-to-serve analysis; weekly business review (WBR)
Leadership Principles this story carries — one-line proof sentence each

Dive Deep I took $0.66 → $0.03 apart: 22.0× = 6.0× fewer containers needing a human (60% → 10%) × 3.67× fewer touches on each one that did — and showed the split depends on the order you attribute it.

Insist on the Highest Standards Seven metric definitions frozen and signed by the owners who would be red against them; a 15-day active denominator; a truncated 99.4% week (three touches) thrown out.

Earn Trust I printed a data miss instead of a flattering number, because three touches is not a week.

Are Right, A Lot Every red was decomposed before the meeting; a −45% week (40.0% on 17 Jan to 21.9% on 31 Jan 2022, record) traced to a carrier format change — measurement, not operators — and the same number was checked the next Monday.

Honesty label. Same program family as S11, a different cut: S11 is the boarding process and its gates; this story is the measurement system and the analysis behind it. The review format was copied from the LSA weekly review, not invented, and I built it by hand every Monday for months. The 22× decomposition and the 56% / 44% attribution are my model on record endpoints — they assume touches fall only on non-automated containers, and I say the split is a convention, not a measurement (the order-dependence is shown in Table 3). The week-over-week values (40.0% on 17 Jan → 21.9% on 31 Jan 2022), the three grains (19.4% · 54.1% · 40.2% → 69.2%) and the discarded 99.4% week are record from my own review workbook (Apple v11 A2, extract SV_28.xlsx); the hand-built review ran for months. The real LSA weekly-review workbook (Table 5) is the format I copied — its FFA tab is another team's slide, used here to show the mechanics on real data, not as my program's numbers. An early draft note in the source folder carries much larger growth multipliers for customers and volume — they conflict with canon (7 → 68 = 9.7×) and are never said.
What the record says about the review (from the program documents; record-from-docs)

The format. The LSA weekly-review instructions separate output from input metrics; a week runs Monday–Sunday; the view carries five consecutive weeks plus months; week-over-week is the relative change (B − A)/A; green is favorable beyond 5%, red unfavorable beyond 5%. record-from-docs

What the program asked of it. The Feb 1, 2022 E-Team takeaways name Goal 1 as lowering cost-to-serve, ask for cost-to-serve measured for 3PL + 4PL versus 4PL only, have the visibility team publish its own weekly review, and set the improvement priority rule: rank carriers, terminals and milestones on the two priority lanes by cost-to-serve reduction first, ease of getting the data second. record-from-docs

What the review ran on. The BRD names the seven metrics and the as-is: a manual Zendesk pull matched to a manual container pull in Google Sheets, 1.5–2 hours per week-over-week snapshot (a BRD measure; my own account is "about four hours a week at its worst" — two different measures, never stacked). My review workbook (Apple A2) is the container-status-history extract SV_28.xlsx — 10,289 events, week ending 28 Feb 2022. record-from-docs

60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Flexport's visibility program was being judged by leadership that said it was not demonstrating value, and the only instrument was a spreadsheet I stitched from four systems every Monday. Cost-to-serve was $0.66 a container; nobody could say whether it would fall because automation covered more containers or because each human touch got cheaper, and one flattering week could steer a rollout. Task. As program owner, make the numbers decision-grade. Action. I copied the LSA weekly review's mechanics — Monday-to-Sunday weeks, five weeks in view, week-over-week as (B − A)/A, red or green beyond ±5% — froze seven metrics with owners who signed the definitions they would be red against, tagged every input to the output it moves, and fixed the denominator: active is the cumulative inflow less containers 15 days past departed terminal. Then I took the headline apart: $0.66 to $0.03 is 22×, which is 6× fewer containers needing a human — 60% down to 10% — times about 3.7× fewer touches on each one that still did. When a week printed 99.4% on three touches I discarded it, because a 95% interval on three is 44% to 100%. Result. Primary metric: cost-to-serve $0.66 → $0.03 per container — about 95% — across customers 7 → 68, with every red arriving with an owner and a dated fix — and the same seven definitions now live in code.

SAFE-SAY — two sentences to say exactly

Most attackable number. “Cost-to-serve went from sixty-six cents to three cents a container — about ninety-five percent. The split into six-times coverage and three-and-two-thirds touches is my model on those two record endpoints, and I call it a convention, not a measurement.”

“What's your source for that?” “The endpoints and the weekly prints come from the review workbook I built — the container-status extract — and from the program's own documents; the split and the sample-size arithmetic are mine, and I label them modeled.”

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — The program had public targets — 68 customers and 170 monthly active users (both landed two months early) and a 32,000-shipment target I managed toward — and leadership that said value was not legible; it was about to drop from #3 to #14 of 16 initiatives. The instrument was my Monday spreadsheet: four systems (tickets, container pulls, platform data, Jira), about four hours a week at its worst, one person who could break it. The analysis problem underneath had three parts: a headline that moved ($0.66 → $0.03) with no explanation of what moved it; denominators that could flatter or flatten a week; and small-N weeks that could mislead a VP.

T — Own the measurement design and the analysis: define what is measured, at which grain, against which denominator, and what the number must be able to explain — so leadership could steer rollout pace and funding off it.

Baselines on the table: CTS $0.66/container (≈3.7 touches at the record $0.18 per interaction) · automation 40% · ETA accuracy 60% · customers 7 · review built by hand — about four hours a week at its worst · seven metrics · five Mon–Sun weeks in view · ±5% week-over-week rule.

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery — documented the as-is (four systems, Sheets, a person-dependent manual build); asked what leadership decides on (rollout pace, funding) and wrote the questions the review must answer weekly; chose the LSA review as the pattern to copy. Exit bar: leadership agrees the weekly questions; each input has a named owner.
  • Plan & Design — froze seven metric definitions (coverage auto, coverage with human, tickets, tickets per active container, 4PL SLA %, 3PL SLA %, delta); input→output tagging; the ±5% week-over-week rule; the 15-day active denominator; a parking lot for new metrics. Exit bar: every owner signs the definition they will be red against; first Monday deck dry-run.
  • Build & Test — built the deck by hand every Monday; pre-decomposed every red into source, definition or operator before the meeting; enforced the data-honesty rule (a truncated week is a data miss, not a result); tied Jira blocker/critical trends to the input metrics. Exit bar: two consecutive Mondays where every red arrives with an owner and a dated fix, and the number verifies the following Monday.
  • Cutover & Go-Live — the Monday review became the forum where rollout decisions were taken; waves boarded only through the published criteria; the decomposition went into the deck so leadership could see which lever moved cost-to-serve. Exit bar: the VP takes the rollout decision in the room off the numbers.
  • Hypercare / Close — discarded the flattering 99.4% three-touch week and printed a data miss; moved the definitions into code with the Snowflake pipeline (S16-A) so the review no longer depended on one person. Exit bar: same seven numbers within 1% for consecutive weeks; the critic steers by them.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the diagnosis, the seven definitions, the denominator rule, the decomposition, the data-honesty rule and the Monday build. The input owners — Data Services, Support, GTM and the engineering teams — signed their own definitions and carried their own reds; the VP decided in the room. The format came from the LSA review.

Designed the process; others own it now. I designed the definitions, the denominator and the reading rules; the owners run their lines, and the pipeline (S16-A) carries the definitions in code. Trade-off put to leadership. Coverage of the page (every metric anyone wanted) versus stability of the page (seven that never change definition) — I recommended stability and gave the rest a parking lot.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality (record vs modeled labeled)
  • $ — cost-to-serve $0.66 → $0.03 per container (record; about 95%); direct dollars ≈$50K/yr modeled at the 32,000-shipment target on an illustrative 2.5 containers per shipment; ≈$21K/yr of by-hand review labor modeled (≈4 h/wk at its worst, at $100/h — an upper bound); the ~$1.5M pre-prod figure (S11, user anchor) stays separate.
  • # — seven frozen metrics, each with a named owner; customers 7 → 68 (9.7×) with zero rollbacks; 68 customers and 170 monthly users landed two months early (record); the 32,000-shipment figure is a target, not a result.
  • % — automation 40% → 90%; ETA accuracy 60% → 90% (record); coverage-versus-touch-efficiency split ≈56% / 44% (modeled).
  • Time — by hand for months — about four hours a week at its worst — until the pipeline matched within 1% (record, Apple A1/A2); rollout unlocked two months early off the review (record).
  • Customer — the review carried the graduation criteria and CSAT ≥ consignee average into the rollout decision — the customer lens was on the Monday page (record).
  • Quality — a truncated 99.4% week (three touches) discarded and printed as a data miss; a −45% week (40.0% → 21.9%, 17 → 31 Jan 2022) traced to a carrier format change, not operators; definitions frozen and later moved into code (record).

Probing — five facets, then the full drill

Facet 1 — Data analysis (what data, grain, joins, cuts, what it revealed)

Grain and joins. The instrument was the container-status-history extract (SV_28.xlsx, week ending 28 Feb 2022): the grain was the container-status event, not the shipment, rolled up to a weekly stock. Three grains, one formula family, never blended: event 1,991 of 10,289 human-touched = 19.4%; ticket queue 538 auto vs 456 human = 54.1% auto; weekly stock (the headline) 40.2% on 13 Dec 2021 → 69.2% on 21 Feb 2022 record (Apple v11 A2). Sources: Zendesk tickets (a ticket is created when Visibility fails to auto-initiate tracking), the container pull from Crux, platform tracking data and Jira blocker/critical counts record-from-docs. The join is ticket ID ↔ container number; tickets carried no container ID, so the first versions matched by hand — the gap S16-A closes with a cross-reference table.

Cuts. Coverage automated vs with human input; tickets and tickets per active container; SLA for 4PL vs 3PL and the delta between them; 3PL + 4PL vs 4PL only (the E-Team ask). Every cut is an output metric tagged to the inputs that move it.

What the analysis revealed. (1) The headline was two multiplied levers: 22.0× = 6.0× (containers needing a human 60% → 10%) × 3.67× (touches per touched container 6.11 → 1.67) — a modeled split on record endpoints. (2) The attribution is order-dependent, so I report the fair split (average of both orders): ≈56% coverage, ≈44% touch efficiency. (3) A −45% week-over-week red — 40.0% on 17 Jan to 21.9% on 31 Jan 2022 — traced to a carrier format change, measurement not operators record. (4) The 21 Feb print of 69.2% moved with the denominator (active book 287 → 546 the week a big client's 283 containers hit), and the 28 Feb print of 99.4% rested on three touches — a truncated week, discarded record.

Facet 4 — Implementation (phases, owners, rollout, cutover, controls, risks)

ControlStandard workOwnerLean / Kaizen word
DefinitionsSeven written definitions, signed, frozen; new metrics enter a parking lot, never the pageEach input ownerStandard work
Weekly buildPull four systems → join ticket ↔ container → seven metrics on the 15-day active base → WoW ±5%Me, by hand, then the pipelineTakt: the Monday cadence
Red handlingDecompose to source / definition / operator before the meeting; owner + dated fix; verify the same number next MondayMetric ownerRoot cause (5 Whys), PDCA
Data honestyA truncated or tiny-N week is a data miss, never a resultMeJidoka — stop the line on bad data
Hand-offPipeline parity within 1% for consecutive weeks, then retire the manual build (S16-A)Infra + meRemove waste (motion) and the single point of failure

Risks and mitigations. Measurement-caused reds (decompose first); a flattering wrong number (data-honesty rule); a single point of failure (the manual review); an expanding page (parking lot). Rollout of the review itself: dry-run deck → two consecutive clean Mondays → the VP takes a decision in the room → the review becomes the standing forum. A review that skips a week teaches leadership it can be skipped, so it ran every Monday.

Facet 5 — Probe questions (hardest follow-ups, numeric answers)

Is the 6.0× / 3.67× split real, or an artifact of your assumption?

It is a model, and I say so. The endpoints are record: $0.66 → $0.03 and coverage 40% → 90%. The split assumes every touch lands on a non-automated container; if exception handling touches automated containers too, the touch-efficiency lever is understated. The honest convention is the average of both attribution orders — 56% coverage, 44% touches — and the order-dependence itself (coverage’s share runs 24%–87%) is why I call it a convention, not a measurement.

How do you know the −45% week was not operators?

Because I decomposed it before the meeting: source, definition, operator, in that order. The drop traced to a carrier format change — measurement. The owner took a dated fix and I looked at the same number the next Monday. The weekly prints were 40.0% on 17 Jan and 21.9% on 31 Jan 2022; the arithmetic is (21.9 − 40.0)/40.0 = −45%.

Why a 15-day active window, and what would change it?

It is the honest denominator for per-container rates — cumulative containers would let the base swamp the signal — in the real extract the active book went 287 → 546 in the week one big client's 283 containers hit, and the headline moved from the low 40s to 69.2% with it. Fifteen days past departed terminal was frozen with the owners. What would change it: showing the rate at 10, 15 and 30 days and freezing the window where the rate stops moving VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH · V2.

Three touches — why discard instead of caveat?

Because the interval was wider than the band. At 3 of 3 the 95% Wilson interval is 0.44 to 1.00 — 0.56 wide — against a ±5% (0.10-wide) red/green band. A number you cannot read against its own threshold is not a reading. To read a rate to ±5 points at 90% you need about 139 active containers.

You froze seven metrics. What did that cost?

Coverage of the page — engineering wanted more end-to-end input metrics, and the review parked them. The trade was stability: a number that changes definition cannot steer a rollout. The parking lot was real, and the pipeline (S16-A) is where the extra inputs could be added without touching the seven.

What decisions did the review actually change?

Three, all record: waves boarded only through the published criteria; wave 3 held in public on an ETA-accuracy miss traced to one bad source; the VP unlocked the 68-client rollout two months early off the review's numbers. The E-Team's improvement rule — rank by cost-to-serve reduction first, ease second — is the same Pareto the reds were sorted by.

|    +- fair split of the $0.63 saving: coverage ~56% / touch efficiency ~44% (modeled)

+- TAGS    Jira blocker/critical trend  <->  the input metric it explains
+- DENOM   active = inflow less containers 15 days past departed terminal; tiny-N weeks = data miss

   -> pipeline parity within 1% for consecutive weeks -> manual build retired
