Total impact statement — say it like this

Cost-to-serve fell from sixty-six cents to three cents a container — twenty-two times. When I took it apart it was two levers multiplied: six times fewer containers needing a human, sixty percent down to ten, and about three and two-thirds times fewer touches on each one that still did. That split is my model — it assumes the touches fall on the non-automated containers — and the attribution depends on the order you take it, so I report the average, roughly fifty-six percent coverage and forty-four percent touch efficiency, and I call it modeled. At the thirty-two-thousand-shipment target the direct saving is about fifty thousand dollars a year on an illustrative container count, plus about twenty-one thousand of review labor I stopped doing by hand — both modeled. What is record is the sixty-six cents, the three cents and seven to sixty-eight; what the review added is that every red arrived with an owner and a dated fix, and that when a week printed ninety-nine point four percent on three touches I printed a data miss instead.

If challenged — the full answers

"Your 22× decomposition is just algebra."

The product identity is algebra; the analysis is knowing it needs an attribution convention. If touches per touched container had stayed flat, the whole result would have been coverage; because both moved, the order matters, so I show both orders and report the average. The use is practical: it tells the review which lever to fund next.

"Why throw out a number that looked good?"

Three touches gives an interval of 44% to 100% — wider than the ±5% band the review reads against. Printing it would have taught the VP a number that could not survive the next Monday, and a review that flatters once is not trusted twice.

"An early note claims a far bigger growth multiple than 7 → 68. Which is it?"

The record is 7 → 68, 9.7×, against public targets of 68 customers and 170 monthly active users — both landed two months early — and a 32,000-shipment tracked target. The larger multiples are from an early draft note and are not canon; I do not use them.

20-second version and NACF bridge
20-second version

At Flexport I ran the weekly review that steered a visibility rollout: seven frozen metrics, a 15-day active denominator, ±5% red/green. I took the 22× cost-to-serve drop apart — 6× fewer containers needing a human, 3.7× fewer touches on each — and threw out a 99.4% week built on three touches.

NACF bridge — one sentence

The same review shape runs an inbound network — doors × hours × unload rate on one side, UPH and cubic fill on the other, each with a frozen weekly definition — so when door utilization crosses the ~85% knee or weeks-of-cover leaves its 2–4 week band, the red arrives with an owner, a dated fix and an honest denominator (doors in use, not doors installed).

Behavioral pack — four questions this story answers, each a separate 90-second STAR from a different facet

B1 · “Tell me about a problem you had to solve that required in-depth thought and analysis. How did you know you were focusing on the right things? What was the outcome? Would you have done anything differently?” AIQB: Dive Deep Q3 Dive Deep facet: data analysis

Open (first two sentences): “A cost-to-serve number fell ninety-five percent and nobody could tell me why, so I took it apart. It was two multiplied levers, not one.”

90-second STAR. S Leadership doubted the program, and the headline was $0.66 → $0.03 per container. T Explain what moved it, so the review knew what to fund next. A I converted dollars to touches at the record $0.18 per interaction — 3.67 down to 0.17 — then split coverage (containers needing a human, 60% → 10%, 6.0×) from touches per touched container (6.11 → 1.67, 3.67×). I noticed the attribution changes with order — $0.55/$0.08 coverage-first, $0.48/$0.15 touches-first — so I reported the average, 56/44. R The split was modeled and I said so; the review had a shared way to talk about where the next fix should go, and the record endpoints stayed untouched.

Land: 22.0× = 6.0× × 3.67×  ·  Pivot to NACF: the same move on dock-to-stock cost: split a throughput gain into door-hours opened and unload rate before deciding which to fund.

Do NOT say: that the 56/44 split was measured; any customer-growth multiple other than 9.7×; any dollar from the split as record.

B2 · “Describe a time when you refused to compromise your standards around quality/customer service, etc. Who was your customer? What was the result?” AIQB: Highest Standards Q1 Insist on the Highest Standards facet: definitions and denominators

Open (first two sentences): “Every owner wanted their metric on the page, and I froze seven and parked the rest. A number that changes definition cannot steer a rollout.”

90-second STAR. S A weekly review drifts: metrics get added, definitions bend, the page stops meaning anything. T Keep the review decision-grade for a VP who would steer funding off it. A I wrote seven definitions, got each input owner to sign the one they would be red against, fixed the denominator at 15-day active, offered a parking lot for new metrics and held the line after sign-off. R Every red arrived with an owner and a dated fix; the same seven definitions later moved into code with no drift.

Land: seven metrics, 15-day active  ·  Pivot to NACF: freeze door-utilization and weeks-of-cover definitions before the quarter, signed by the node owner, so a red means the same thing in every building.

Do NOT say: "I invented the WBR" — it was copied from the LSA review, which was the point.

B3 · “Tell me about a time when you didn't have enough data to make the right decision. What did you do? What path did you take? Did the decision turn out to be the correct one?” AIQB: Are Right, A Lot Q1 Earn Trust facet: data honesty

Open (first two sentences): “I had a week that printed ninety-nine point four percent and I threw it out. It rested on three touches, so I printed a data miss in its place.”

90-second STAR. S The program was being doubted, and a flattering week landed. T Protect leadership's trust in the number over the next month, not the next meeting. A I computed the interval: 3 of 3 gives a 95% Wilson interval of 0.44 to 1.00 — wider than the ±5% band. I told the VP it was a data miss, not a result. R The number never had to be walked back, and the critic who started this later steered by the review.

Land: N = 3 → interval 44%–100%  ·  Pivot to NACF: a unit-fill or WOC reading built on a handful of trailers or SKUs gets marked insufficient volume, not headlined.

Do NOT say: what the "real" number was — I do not have one and will not invent it.

B4 · “Tell me about a situation that required you to dig deep to get to the root cause. How did you know you were focusing on the right things? What was the outcome? Would you have done anything differently?” AIQB: Dive Deep Q2 Are Right, A Lot facet: root cause on measurement

Open (first two sentences): “The weekly automation rate fell from forty percent on the seventeenth of January to twenty-one point nine on the thirty-first — minus forty-five, deep red. Before the meeting I decomposed it, and it was a carrier format change, not the operators.”

90-second STAR. S A red week landed on a desk that could have been blamed. T Find the cause before anyone defended or accused. A The standard work: source, definition, operator, in that order. The source had changed format; I assigned the owner a dated fix and checked the same number the next Monday. R The red arrived with an owner, the operators were not blamed, and the order of questions became standard work.

Land: (21.9 − 40.0)/40.0 = −45% — printed weekly values 17 Jan → 31 Jan 2022 (record)  ·  Pivot to NACF: a sudden UPH or weeks-of-cover swing gets the same three questions — source, definition, operator — before an ops root cause is declared.

Do NOT say: the carrier name; that operators had slipped.

Same story, three angles — three cards, three openers, three metrics, three LPs

Data / analytical angle Dive Deep

Opener: “Cost-to-serve fell 22× and I can tell you which lever did it — and why the answer depends on the order you ask.”

Headline metric: 22.0× = 6.0× coverage × 3.67× touch efficiency; fair split 56% / 44% (modeled)

  • Grain: container-week; join ticket ↔ container; 15-day active base.
  • Identity: 6.0 × 3.67 = 22.0.
  • Order test: $0.55/$0.08 coverage-first vs $0.48/$0.15 touches-first.
  • Say "modeled" once, early; the record is the endpoints.

Conflict / influence / backbone angle Insist on the Highest Standards

Opener: “The owners wanted their metric on the page. I held seven and gave them a parking lot — and a signature line.”

Headline metric: seven frozen metrics, each signed by the owner who would be red against it

  • The objection: more end-to-end input metrics, now.
  • The answer: stability over coverage; a changing definition cannot steer a rollout.
  • The mechanism: sign-off, freeze, parking lot — no argument by seniority.
  • The proof: the same definitions moved into code with no drift.

Honest failure-and-learning angle Ownership

Opener: “I started the review after the critique, not at kickoff — and for months I was its single point of failure.”

Headline metric: by hand for months, about four hours a week at its worst; no longer a single point of failure once the pipeline matched within 1%

  • What I would do differently: instrument at kickoff, not after the program fell from #3 to #14 of 16.
  • What I would add now: a written sample floor (~139 active containers for ±5 points at 90%) instead of a judgment call on each small week.
  • What fixed it: definitions in code (S16-A), not heroics.
  • No dollar claimed for this angle.
Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups

"How do you know the drop was your review and not the automation teams — or seasonality?"

Numeric answer. I do not claim the review built the automation; nine teams did (coverage 40% → 90%). The review's claim is narrower: it made boarding follow coverage — 9.7× the customers, zero rollbacks, both targets two months early. Cost-to-serve is a per-container ratio, so seasonal volume does not move it; a mix shift could, and I would check that by lane.

Say exactly: “I don't claim the review made the automation — the teams built that. The review's claim is narrower and checkable: it made boarding follow coverage, and the record shows zero rollbacks at 9.7 times the customers.”

"Your decomposition rests on an assumption. What number would change your mind?"

Numeric answer. The endpoints do not depend on it; only the split does. Depending on attribution order coverage's share runs $0.15 to $0.55 of the $0.63 saving — 24% to 87% — so no single split is a measurement. Ticket-grain data showing touches on automated containers would move me to a two-factor model with an interaction term.

Say exactly: “The endpoints are record; only the split is modeled — and the split runs from twenty-four to eighty-seven percent depending on order, so I report the average and call it a convention.”

"Three touches — why not just report it with a caveat?"

Numeric answer. The 95% interval was 0.44 to 1.00 — 0.56 wide — against a 0.10-wide red/green band, 5.6 times wider than the threshold. A caveat does not fix a reading the threshold cannot interpret. To read a rate to ±5 points at 90% I need about 139 active containers.

Say exactly: “A number whose interval is wider than the band you read it against isn't a reading. At three touches the interval was forty-four to a hundred; to read ninety percent within five points you need about a hundred and forty.”

S11-B — Flexport — Launch readiness as standard work: four test gates, a 100-container hop-by-hop ledger and six graduation criteria — and the pilot that launched in December 2021, later than the first testing plan NEW

LPs this story can serve here: Insist on the Highest Standards (primary) · Customer Obsession · Dive Deep · Learn and Be Curious (the honest miss)
Process family: Deliver — launch readiness, test strategy and staged rollout; entry / exit criteria and go / no-go governance
Leadership Principles this story carries — one-line proof sentence each

Insist on the Highest Standards Business UAT did not open until 80% of shipments showed minor-to-no issues, and no wave graduated until six published criteria passed — the gate was data quality, not the calendar.

Customer Obsession Shadow accounts ran two real Crux clients' live data beside the old product, and every new client supplied at least 50 containers of its own freight to calibrate on before onboarding.

Dive Deep The 100-container ledger traced real containers hop by hop; of 59 failing rows, 41 (69.5%) first broke at route creation or the milestone on screen — two hops, not seven.

Learn and Be Curious My test-effort estimates were optimistic and monitoring arrived late, so the pilot launched later than the first testing plan and bugs took hours to localize across six-plus layers — the lesson is to size testing on hop count and instrument first.

Honesty label. One of two technical program managers on the program (the record also names a co-TPM and a DRI). I owned the gate definitions, the readiness read-out and the go/no-go inputs; the 100-container log was run day to day by the QA team named in the testing approach, the hop-by-hop design is credited in the secondary loop packet to a test lead, and business users ran UAT. My own account (Apple v11 A5) is that I ran decision week as a two-gate go/no-go with criteria published beforehand, made the 100-container run the UAT spine with a Jira ID on every missed row, and set the UAT entrance criteria as exposure gates; I say "I defined the gates and ran the read-out", not "I ran the test". The ledger numbers below are my recount of the 100-container working log — a partial log where blank means not recorded. Timing, doc against doc: the testing-approach document planned an early-November pilot; the program's year-end summary shows launch in December 2021 with delays among its misses; the pilot roll-out schedule has Wave 1 in Q4 2021, and my own workbook calls go-live "on schedule". I therefore say "later than the first testing plan", never "on time" and never a specific number of weeks late.
What the record says about readiness (from the program documents; record-from-docs)

Four test gates. A "100 container test" (random container and MBL numbers traced and logged, bugs in Jira, daily stand-ups); bug bashes every other week with job-story ranges assigned per person; shadow client accounts (client data uploaded to the legacy product fed into dedicated demo accounts, starting with two named Crux clients); and UAT with semi-weekly syncs, dependent on data quality first — 80% of shipments with minor-to-no issues. record-from-docs

Go / no-go and waves. Two go/no-go meetings (business and tech) read: stable production, first-client data quality 80%+, no show-stoppers. Waves then graduated on six criteria — verification SLA 90% (max 48 h in "Verification in progress"), data quality 90%+ at least as accurate as the legacy product, milestone estimates 80%+, stable production, at least 100 containers plus key features used, and CSAT on par with the consignee average — with at least 50 containers of a new client's own data to calibrate on first, a free pilot and light-weight onboarding. record-from-docs

Where it landed. The Nov 2021 status read Yellow with both checkpoints passed, a performance test of 500 containers in 20 minutes at 99.6% connectivity accuracy, end-to-end 100% processed and 86% trackable on 11/18, job stories 98.4% complete. The year-end summary: launched in December 2021, nine teams and close to 100 people, three environments built — and the misses: delays, optimistic testing estimates, and monitoring implemented late (bugs took hours to localize across six-plus system layers). record-from-docs

60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Flexport's shipment-visibility pilot was first planned for early November 2021 and launched in December. It ran on a new stack across nine teams and close to a hundred people, with data crossing six or more system layers before it reached a client's screen. Task. As one of the program's two TPMs, I owned the readiness gates: what had to be true before a client touched the product, and before each wave graduated. Action. We tested in four layers — a 100-container live test, biweekly bug bashes, shadow accounts that ran two real clients' data beside the old product, and business UAT that opened only when 80% of shipments showed minor-to-no issues. The 100-container ledger traced real containers hop by hop — created, subscribed, data back, route, milestone, exception, webhook — and its Pareto was blunt: of 59 failing rows, 41 first failed at route creation or the milestone on screen. Graduation was six published criteria, with at least 50 containers of the client's own freight to calibrate on first. Result. Primary metric: the gate held — a 500-container load test in 20 minutes at 99.6% connectivity, both go/no-go checkpoints passed, no wave rolled back. The honest part: my test-effort estimates were optimistic and monitoring arrived late, so the pilot launched later than first planned and bugs took hours to localize; I now size testing on hop count and put the monitors in first.

SAFE-SAY — two sentences to say exactly

Most attackable number. “Of fifty-nine failing rows in the hundred-container log, forty-one — about seventy percent — first broke at two hops, route creation and the milestone on screen; that is my recount of a working log, so it tells you where to look, not a defect rate.”

“What's your source for that?” “The pre-launch evidence is in the program's own status documents — five hundred containers in twenty minutes at ninety-nine point six, the dry run a hundred percent processed and eighty-six tracked — and the hop recount is mine from the log file.”

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — The visibility pilot had to go live to Crux-grade clients on a brand-new stack: nine teams, close to 100 people, three environments, data traveling through six-plus system layers before it reached the Client App. The testing plan targeted an early-November pilot; the status record shows end of November and the year-end summary shows December 2021 (the roll-out schedule keeps Wave 1 inside Q4, and my own workbook records go-live on schedule — the documents disagree on whether December was late). The program's own retrospective calls the testing estimates "overly optimistic for the complexity of the product" and names monitoring as implemented too late.

T — Define "ready": the gates before a client touches the product, the criteria to graduate each wave, and the evidence the go/no-go checkpoints would read — so rollout followed data quality, not the calendar.

Baselines on the table: First testing plan early Nov 2021 → launched Dec 2021 (docs disagree whether late) · 9 teams / ~100 people · 6+ system layers · 3 environments · 4 test gates · 6 graduation criteria · ≥50 containers per new client to calibrate · UAT entry gate 80% minor-to-no issues · ledger: 474 containers listed, 142 marked tested, 105 with hop results.

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery — defined "working" per container as seven checks (created, subscribed, data back, route, milestone, exception, webhook); read the product standards (coverage 90%+, accuracy 95%, timeliness 90%) and what each go/no-go would read. Exit bar: seven checks and four gates named; the questions the go/no-go answers written down.
  • Plan & Design — four test gates; the UAT entry gate at 80% minor-to-no issues; six graduation criteria published before wave 1; at least 50 containers of each new client's own freight before onboarding; free pilot, light-weight onboarding, monthly go/no-go. Exit bar: criteria published to Sales and the business before wave 1.
  • Build & Test — the 100-container log in daily stand-ups; bug bashes by job-story range; shadow accounts on real Crux data; production connectivity monitors for the hops; a trace ID and per-hop ratios proposed after the slow bug hunts — the logs showed no unique ID tying events from downstream to the screen, so finding the source system took 2–4 hours per blocker; the recommendation cut blocker-bug time-to-resolve by about half (3PL ingestion one-pager). Exit bar: 500 containers / 20 minutes / 99.6%; end-to-end 100% processed, 86% trackable on 11/18.
  • Cutover & Go-Live — two go/no-go checkpoints (business, tech) on stable production, first-client data quality 80%+ and no show-stoppers; wave 1 started in December with two hand-carried customers (the schedule and my workbook agree on those two; two other accounts ran as shadow UAT accounts, and the Nov 19 status note's "first pilot client" is the earlier plan — I say "the first two hand-carried customers", no names, unless asked). Exit bar: both checkpoints passed; wave 1 live.
  • Hypercare / Close — feedback session about a week after kickoff; a 50-container calibration sample from each wave-2 client before onboarding; monthly go/no-go on the six criteria; production data-quality testing on the same per-hop ratios. Exit bar: wave 2 opened on wave 1's graduation read; zero rollbacks.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the definition of ready, the gate and criteria design, the go/no-go inputs and the pre-production measurement design (S11). The QA team ran the log, a test lead designed the hop columns, the engineering teams instrumented their hops and fixed what the log found, business users ran UAT, and product and engineering leadership took the go/no-go.

Designed the process; others own it now. The gates and criteria are published artifacts, the ledger columns are the standard checklist, and the per-hop ratios became standing monitors — the launch reads off documents, not off me. Trade-off put to leadership. Launch date versus data quality: I recommended the data-quality gate and accepted the later date it implied.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality (record vs modeled labeled)
  • $ — no record dollar for the testing layer. The ~$1.5M pre-production estimate is S11's separate user anchor and is never merged. An illustrative escape cost at the record $0.18 per touch is under $1K a year — the real cost of an escaped defect is the rollback and the first impression, which is what the gates bought.
  • # — four gates; six graduation criteria; 500 containers in 20 minutes at 99.6%; end-to-end 11/18 100% processed, 86% trackable; job stories 98.4% complete; both go/no-go checkpoints passed (record-from-docs).
  • % — UAT entry gate 80% minor-to-no issues; graduation bars 90% data quality, 80% milestone estimates, 90% verification SLA; ledger first-failure Pareto 69.5% in two hops (my read of the log).
  • Time — pilot launched December 2021, later than the early-November testing plan (record-from-docs; the docs disagree whether it was late); bug localization took hours across six-plus layers (record); about half that after the trace ID (3PL ingestion one-pager: blocker-bug time-to-resolve −50%).
  • Customer — shadow accounts on two real clients' data; ≥50 containers of each new client's freight to calibrate on; CSAT par with the consignee average inside the graduation gate (record-from-docs).
  • Quality — zero rollbacks through the waves (record, S11 canon); every ledger miss ticketed to the owning team.

Probing — five facets, then the full drill

Facet 1 — Data analysis (what data, grain, joins, cuts, what it revealed)

What the ledger is. One row per container or MBL; one column per hop — created, subscribed in SV, data back, route created, milestone shown, exception shown, webhook created — plus an overall Success flag, a date and the bug IDs record-from-docs. Population as I read it: 474 containers listed, 142 marked tested, 105 with at least one hop result, 53 with an overall verdict (19 Yes, 34 No) — a partial working log; blank means not recorded, and twelve numbered issues sit in the bug column.

The right Pareto is first-failing hop, not No-counts per column. Downstream hops cascade — a failed route makes the milestone fail — so counting every "No" double-counts one fault. Of the 105 rows with results, 59 had at least one "No"; each was assigned to the first hop in order that failed:

First failing hopRowsShare of 59Cumulative
Route created?2339.0%39.0%
Milestone shown in UI?1830.5%69.5%
Data back from the legacy source711.9%81.4%
Exception shown in UI?610.2%91.5%
Shipment created?58.5%100.0%

What it revealed. Two hops — route creation and the milestone on screen — hold 41 of 59 first failures (69.5%); the ingestion hops (created, data back) hold 12 (20.3%). Only 3 of the 105 rows passed all seven hops. So the defects were downstream of ingestion, which is where the bug bashes and the owning teams went first. Caveats I would say out loud: this is a working log, not a random sample, and I would re-run a fresh random 100 and log all seven hops to turn the Pareto into a defect-rate estimate. The Pareto is my recount of the 100-Container Debugging Session log, not a number printed in any document.

UAT entryOpen on the calendar (mid-October) · open on a data-quality gate (80% minor-to-no issues)Quality gateBusiness testers should validate the product, not rediscover known bugs (record-from-docs).

Risks and mitigations. Environment outages (three environments; a "path to green" process to protect UAT availability); late monitoring (per-hop ratios and connectivity monitors — built later than they should have been); optimistic estimates (the lesson). Lean / Kaizen mapping. The ledger is genchi genbutsu — trace the real unit; the UAT gate is jidoka — stop the line on data quality; the criteria are standard work; the monthly go/no-go is PDCA at wave grain; defects found at the bottom cost a ticket, found by a client they cost trust.

Facet 5 — Probe questions (hardest follow-ups, numeric answers)

Was the pilot late, and whose miss was it?

The first testing plan said early November and it launched in December; the program's retrospective lists delays, optimistic testing estimates and late monitoring, and I was one of its two TPMs, so I own my share of the estimate. I won't say how much was mine against the co-TPM's — I wasn't the only estimator. I now size test effort on hop count and put the monitors in before the debugging starts.

What does your ledger actually prove?

Where to look first, not a defect rate. It is a partial working log: 142 marked tested, 105 with results, 59 failing. By first-failing hop, route (23) and milestone (18) hold 41 of 59 — 69.5% — and only 3 of 105 passed all seven hops. To estimate a rate I would re-run a fresh random 100 and log every hop.

Why is entry at 80% but graduation at 90%?

The bars answer different questions. Entry (first-client data quality 80%+, no show-stoppers) says "safe enough for a calibrated client"; graduation (90%+ at least as accurate as the legacy product, 80%+ milestone estimates, 90% SLA) says "proven enough for the next wave" (record-from-docs).

Why at least 50 containers to calibrate?

Because 50 detects a 5% defect 92.3% of the time (1 − 0.95^50), and 100 detects it 99.4% (1 − 0.95^100). Fifty is the cheapest sample that gives the data team a verified baseline on the client's own lanes before onboarding.

How did you decide UAT could open?

On the 80% minor-to-no-issues gate: business testers are further from product development, so opening earlier would have had them rediscovering known bugs. The gate was data quality, not the calendar — which is also why it moved the date.

Is 500 containers in 20 minutes a lot?

25 containers a minute; 99.6% connected is 498 of 500. At the S11 illustrative 80,000 containers a year (≈219 a day) one 20-minute run is ≈2.3 days of average volume — headroom, not proof of peak behavior, and I said so.

What would you change about the testing plan?

Monitors before debugging, effort sized on hop count, the trace ID agreed in design rather than after the first slow bug hunts, and the three environments treated as the critical path — the record's own list of what went wrong.

+- GATE 2  bug bashes every other week: job-story ranges per person

Total impact statement — say it like this

Honestly, the testing layer has no record dollar and I don't invent one. What it has is a gate that held: a hundred-container live test traced hop by hop, shadow accounts on real client data, UAT that stayed closed until eighty percent of shipments showed minor-to-no issues, and six published criteria for each wave — with a fifty-container calibration first, which detects a five-percent defect more than nine times in ten. The load proof was five hundred containers in twenty minutes at ninety-nine point six percent, both go/no-go checkpoints passed, and no wave rolled back. The ledger told us where to look — forty-one of fifty-nine failing containers broke first at route or milestone — and it is a working log, so I use it to aim the fix, not to estimate a rate. The honest cost is the schedule: my testing estimate was optimistic and monitoring came late, so the pilot launched in December, later than the first testing plan. I would take that trade again, and I would instrument first.

If challenged — the full answers

"You had zero rollbacks — isn't that just luck with only three waves?"

Zero events in three waves proves little statistically — the 95% upper bound on a per-wave rollback rate is 1 − 0.05^(1/3) = 63%. The evidence is the gates' detection power (92.3% at 50 containers for a 5% defect, 95.2% at 100 for 3%) and that exposure was bounded: wave 1 was two clients, not the whole seven-client pool.

"Why trust a log with blanks?"

I don't use it as a sample. I use it to rank where to look: 59 failing rows, first failure at route or milestone in 41. To estimate a rate I would re-run a random 100 with every hop recorded.

"What did the later launch cost?"

The record does not price it and the pilot was free, so there was no revenue delay to claim. The cost was engineering capacity, client expectation and credibility — I do not put a number of weeks on it, because the documents disagree — which is why the lesson is about estimates and monitoring, not about the gate.

20-second version and NACF bridge
20-second version

At Flexport I defined "ready" for a visibility launch: four test gates — a hop-by-hop 100-container ledger, bug bashes, shadow accounts on real client data and UAT that opened only at 80% data quality — and six graduation criteria per wave. The gates held and nothing rolled back; the honest miss was optimistic test estimates and late monitoring, which put the launch in December, later than the first testing plan.

NACF bridge — one sentence

A new inbound node launches the same way: trace real units hop by hop (ASN → appointment → door → receive → putaway → available), publish the entry gate and a calibration volume per node before the first trailer, and put the monitors in before the first week of debugging — doors × hours × unload rate only means something once each hop is measured.

Behavioral pack — four questions this story answers, each a separate 90-second STAR from a different facet

B1 · “Tell me about a time when you had to make a decision between standards and delivery. What tradeoffs did you have make? What was the outcome? Knowing what you know now, would you have done anything differently?” AIQB: Highest Standards Q4 Insist on the Highest Standards facet: the UAT gate

Open (first two sentences): “We kept business UAT closed until eighty percent of shipments showed minor-to-no issues. Opening it on the calendar would have had business testers rediscovering bugs we already knew about.”

90-second STAR. S A new-stack launch with a calendar everyone wanted. T Make "ready" a rule, not a feeling. A I helped define four gates and set UAT's entry at 80% minor-to-no issues, with graduation criteria for each wave published before wave 1; the first-client go/no-go read stable production, 80%+ data quality and no show-stoppers. R Both checkpoints passed and nothing rolled back — and the price was real: the pilot launched in December, later than the first testing plan.

Land: UAT entry 80%; six graduation criteria  ·  Pivot to NACF: a new node does not take inbound until its entry gate is met — doors staffed, WMS receiving verified, first trailers traced — whatever the launch calendar says.

Do NOT say: that the later date was "worth it" without naming the cost; any dollar for the gate.

A Shadow accounts fed the legacy-product uploads of two named clients into dedicated demo accounts; wave-2 clients supplied a 50-container sample for calibration; the six criteria included CSAT on par with the consignee average. R Defects surfaced in our accounts, not theirs; the onboarding was light-weight because the product had calibrated on their freight.

Land: ≥50 containers → 92.3% chance to detect a 5% defect  ·  Pivot to NACF: a node's first inbound sample — a trial volume per node — calibrates receiving and putaway before the node takes real peak volume.

Do NOT say: client names beyond what is confirmed; "customers loved it" — the record has no such claim.

I took the top two to the owning teams first and kept the caveat — it is a working log, not a sample. R The fixes were aimed at route and milestone first; the log became the standing checklist.

90-second STAR. S A launch across nine teams on a new stack. T Land a safe pilot; the estimate was part of the job. A I held the data-quality gate and accepted the later date rather than launch on the plan. Same story, three angles — three cards, three openers, three metrics, three LPs

Data / analytical angle Dive Deep

Opener: “Of fifty-nine failing containers, forty-one first broke at one of two hops — and only three of a hundred and five passed all seven.”

Headline metric: 41 of 59 (69.5%) at route + milestone; 3 of 105 all-seven-clean

  • Order: route 23, milestone 18, data back 7, exception 6, created 5.
  • Caveat first: partial log, blank = not recorded.
  • Next step: a fresh random 100 with every hop logged.

Conflict / influence / backbone angle Ownership

Opener: “Nine teams, none reporting to me, each with its own backlog — I had to get every one to instrument its own hop in the middle of a launch crunch.”

Headline metric: nine teams aligned on one seam; time to localize cut about 50% (secondary source)

  • The seam nobody owned: no unique ID from downstream events to the screen.
  • The ask was framed in each team's own bug queue, not the program's need.
  • The sell was arithmetic: a few hours per blocker, every team paying it.
  • Source: the 3PL ingestion one-pager (TPM across 6 systems and 9 teams; no unique ID; 2–4 hours to find the source system per blocker; blocker-bug time-to-resolve cut about 50%).

Honest failure-and-learning angle Learn and Be Curious

Opener: “The first testing plan said early November and we launched in December, and the program's own retrospective says why: testing estimates were optimistic and monitoring was late.”

Headline metric: first plan early November → launched December 2021; hours to localize a bug at six-plus layers

  • What I would do differently: size testing on hop count; monitors before debugging.
  • What the record calls a win, I read as a warning: the environment was built "through long hours" — optimistic estimates are paid for by people.
  • What held: the gates — no wave rolled back.
  • No dollar claimed.
Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups

"With only three waves, how do you know your gates caused zero rollbacks — and not luck?"

Numeric answer. I don't, statistically: zero events in three waves gives a 95% upper bound of 1 − 0.05^(1/3) = 63% on a per-wave rollback rate. The case rests on detection power — 92.3% at 50 containers for a 5% defect, 95.2% at 100 for 3% — and on bounded exposure: wave 1 was two clients, not seven.

Say exactly: “Zero in three waves proves almost nothing on its own — the upper bound is sixty-three percent. What I can show is the gates' detection power and that wave one was two clients, not the whole pool.”

"Your ledger is a partial log. Why should I trust the Pareto?"

Numeric answer. Because I only claim what it supports: 105 rows had results, 59 had a failing hop, 41 of those first failed at route or milestone. Blank is not "pass," and a working log over-records failures, so the shares are indicative; the robust finding is that failures sat downstream of ingestion. I would re-run a random 100 with all seven hops logged.

Say exactly: “It's a working log, not a sample — blank means not recorded. I use it to decide where to look first, not to estimate a defect rate.”

"The pilot launched later than planned. What number would have told you earlier?"

Numeric answer. Hours to localize a blocker: the record says bugs took hours across six-plus layers (2–4 h in the secondary telling). A weekly chart of that figure against a ≤1 h target, from week one, would have shown the monitoring gap before the date did.

Say exactly: “The number I should have charted from week one was hours to localize a blocker — it was the leading indicator, and the date was the lagging one.”

S11-C — Flexport — Retiring an acquired product's book: a segment-by-segment RAPID decision for 79 Crux customers — 55 onto Shipment Visibility, 24 on rule-based exits, and three customers carrying 75% of the containers NEW

LPs this story can serve here: Customer Obsession (primary) · Success and Scale Bring Broad Responsibility · Earn Trust · Have Backbone, Disagree and Commit
Process family: Deliver — customer transition and product retirement; segmentation, decision rights (RAPID) and cut-over planning
Leadership Principles this story carries — one-line proof sentence each

Customer Obsession Every one of the 79 customers got a destination by segment — transition, partner route, or API-or-cancel with dates — instead of an end-of-life email.

Success and Scale Bring Broad Responsibility Three truck brokers were 3.8% of customers and 75.0% of containers, so they got a per-customer partner route; 39 small accounts at ≈$770 a year each got the standard path.

Earn Trust The decision document kept the dissent on the page — cost of two systems, an unwritten parity list, "ejecting customers is never a good experience" — and cancellations ran on published dates.

Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit The decider committed the organization with the objections recorded, and each impacted party committed to perform — nobody smoothed the disagreement away.

Honesty label. This is a decision-document story, and the seat matters: the RAPID table names the Recommend, Agree, Perform, Input and Decide people, and the decider is an executive — the decision was not mine. My seat, from my own workbook (Apple v11 A4): I owned the mitigation of the decommission risk — I put it on the risk register as the top risk, rebuilt the comparison apples to apples, priced the 79-customer base per segment, and wrote the one-canonical-source rule; the transition document's owners built the per-customer paths with me, and the decision itself was the executive decider's. The segment table is the doc's; the footing, concentration arithmetic, break-even rule and parity matrix are my analysis. Footing flag: the doc's printed total row ($15,491 MRR / $185,892 ARR) equals the sum of only the six non-transition segments; all nine rows sum to $21,246 MRR / $254,952 ARR, and the appendix lists 77 names against 79 counted (37 SMB names vs 39) The number I say in the room is the printed one — 79 customers, $185,892 ARR, 294,581 containers (Apple A4 prints the same) — and I add that the printed total equals six of the nine rows. Overlap: the loop packet tells this as S10 for another interviewer.
What the record says (from the program documents; record-from-docs)

The opportunity. Migrate Crux clients to Shipment Visibility by the end of 2022. GKA, Enterprise and SMB customers transition and are upsell opportunities; for Motor Carriers, Truck Brokers, Freight Forwarders, Customs Brokers, 3PL and SCM SaaS the choice was cancel now, or keep serving them on Crux while working out missing features, support and Data Services cost-to-serve. record-from-docs

The decisions. Truck brokers and motor carriers: transition AV Logistics and Loadsmart to partners in Transmission (like ITS, already there); cancel the remaining motor carriers at wind-down. Freight forwarders, customs brokers, 3PL: no new investment — recommend the SV API (no extra dev), else cancel at wind-down. SCM SaaS: cancel both — dated August 1 and October 24, 2022; the customs broker was cancelled September 1, 2022. A named executive decides; two people build the per-segment transition plans and review them with the exec team. record-from-docs

The dissent. One reviewer wanted the cost of maintaining two systems and the stalled "Crux Absorption" project brought into the decision; another asked what Crux costs to keep running, whether the missing-feature list is written down, and warned that ejecting customers is never a good experience and that cancellations should be timed to win the competitors' consignees; others argued to cut direct competitors and to keep only partners. The 2021 plan adds that about 75% of Crux revenue came from clients who are not trade clients, so Crux's front end could not be sunset until the VaaS portal was stood up. record-from-docs

60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Flexport had acquired a visibility product, Crux, and the goal was to move its clients to Shipment Visibility by the end of 2022 — but 79 paying customers and about 295 thousand containers a year were on it, and not all of them fit. The easy versions were an end-of-life email or running two systems forever. Task. Produce a decision rule per customer type that a decider could commit the company to, with the dissent visible. Action. I segmented the 79 into nine types and read each on three questions: does Shipment Visibility serve them, are they a partner or a competitor, and does serving them need extra build. Then I checked concentration: three truck brokers were 4% of customers and 75% of containers, so they got a per-customer partner route; the 39 small accounts, about $770 a year each, got the standard path. The decision: 55 customers to Shipment Visibility, three brokers to partners in Transmission, forwarders and 3PLs to the SV API or cancel at wind-down, nine motor carriers cancel at wind-down, and three dated cancellations. The dissent — cost of two systems, an unwritten parity list — stayed on the page. Result. Primary metric: 79 of 79 customers with a destination and a date — 55 + 3 + 9 + 9 + 3 — and a table footed by container share, because the printed ARR total summed only six of nine rows.

SAFE-SAY — two sentences to say exactly

Most attackable number. “The document prints seventy-nine customers, $185,892 of annual revenue and 294,581 containers; I add that the printed revenue total equals six of its nine segment rows, so I lead with containers.”

“What's your source for that?” “The base is the transition document's own table, which my workbook also quotes; the footing, the three-brokers concentration and the break-even are my arithmetic on that table, and I label them that way.”

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — Flexport's acquired visibility product, Crux, still billed 79 customers — 294,581 containers in 2021 — while Shipment Visibility was in pilot. The aim was to migrate Crux clients by the end of 2022, but the decision document itself says not all customers fit the new service and a plan was needed. Keeping both meant two systems, double maintenance and a prospect set comparing Flexport against itself; closing Crux carelessly meant stranding customers who used it as infrastructure — and the plan's own dependency was that non-trade clients could not use the Client App at all until the VaaS portal existed.

T — Own the segmentation analysis and the per-segment recommendation a decider could commit to — transition, partner route, API-or-cancel, or cancel, each with a date — with the cost of two systems and the parity gaps on the page, not behind it.

Baselines on the table: 79 customers · 294,581 containers (2021) · nine segments · doc-printed ARR total $185,892 (six non-transition rows) vs nine-row sum $254,952 · 12 customers flagged API · 6 flagged churned in the appendix · goal: migrate by end of 2022 · ~75% of Crux revenue from non-trade clients (2021 plan).

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery — footed the customer table (count, containers, ARR, MRR) and read each segment's use, revenue and containers; separated partners from competitors; listed what the plan itself said Crux customers could not do on SV. Exit bar: segment table reconciled to the appendix names; the footing differences written down.
  • Plan & Design — a three-axis rule — does SV serve them, partner or competitor, extra build needed — mapped to five decisions; concentration analysis (brokers vs the long tail); the parity matrix drafted; the open questions listed (keep-alive cost, written parity list). Exit bar: RAPID roles named: Recommend, Agree, Perform, Input, Decide.
  • Build & Test — collected stakeholder input verbatim, including the dissent; ran per-customer conversations for the large accounts with the trucking group; drafted the per-segment transition plans. Exit bar: the decider can read every objection beside the recommendation.
  • Cutover & Go-Live — the decider committed the organization; each impacted party committed to perform; the SV-bound segments entered through the pilot waves; competing SCM SaaS and the customs broker were cancelled on dates. Exit bar: dated actions on the page: August 1, September 1 and October 24, 2022.
  • Hypercare / Close — API-or-cancel conversations for forwarders and 3PLs; partner conversations for the brokers; wind-down of the Crux front end as VaaS / API paths landed; churn flagged in the appendix tracked. Exit bar: every customer on a path with a date; Crux wound down by the end-2022 goal.

Influence and ownership. Mine (as I would tell it): the segmentation, the concentration read, the plan inputs and the parity questions. Not mine: the recommendation owners, the decider and the per-segment transition plans' authors named in the RAPID table; the trucking group owned the partner conversations. I influenced by putting the numbers and the dissent on one page.

Designed the process; others own it now. The segment rules, the dated cancellations and the parity questions live in the decision document, not in my head; the transition plans have named performers. Trade-off put to leadership. Revenue surrendered (the six non-SV segments, $185,892 of printed ARR) against two systems, double maintenance and competing with ourselves — and a keep-alive cost the record never priced.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality (record vs modeled labeled)
  • $ — the doc's printed $185,892 ARR is the revenue on the six non-SV segments (modeled footing — mine); the three SV-bound segments add $69,060, for $254,952 across all nine rows. No Crux keep-alive cost exists in the record, and I do not invent one — the break-even is $15,491 a month (modeled).
  • # — 79 of 79 customers with a decision: 55 to SV, 3 to Transmission partners, 9 SV-API-or-cancel, 9 cancel at wind-down, 3 cancelled now on dates (record-from-docs; tally mine).
  • % — brokers 3.8% of customers and 75.0% of containers; SV-bound segments 69.6% of customers, 13.3% of containers, 27.1% of ARR (my footing of the doc table).
  • Time — target: migrate Crux clients by end of 2022; cancellations dated August 1, September 1 and October 24, 2022 (record-from-docs).
  • Customer — each customer type has a stated destination; the largest accounts got partner conversations, not a template; six customers were already flagged churned in the appendix.
  • Quality — two open questions flagged rather than hidden — keep-alive cost and a written feature-parity list; the doc's own footing differences caught and reconciled.

(6) Flags: 12 customers carry an API flag (5 of them SV-bound) and 6 are flagged churned. I quote 79 customers as printed (Apple A4 prints 79 too); I do not quote a per-customer average beyond the SMB tail.

Controls and risks. Commitment step: each impacted party commits to perform, verbally in the decision meeting or by email. Risks named in the record: cost of two systems and the stalled Crux Absorption project; an unwritten parity list; legal concerns over web scraping; cancellations that hand a competitor's consignees to someone else unless timed. Lean / Kaizen mapping. Segmenting by what flows through the process is value-stream thinking; the dated cancellations are standard work with a due date; the open questions are the PDCA "Check" I would run first.

Facet 5 — Probe questions (hardest follow-ups, numeric answers)

Why not cancel everyone who is not on SV and be done?

Because the three brokers are 75.0% of containers and 54.7% of nine-row ARR — ITS already uses Transmission and the decision routes AV Logistics and Loadsmart there too. Cancelling them would strand the largest flows to save a template. The other 21 customers are exactly the ones the decision exits or re-routes.

What does it cost to keep Crux alive?

The record has no figure — a reviewer asked the question and it is open. I will not invent one; I give the break-even: the printed total of $185,892 a year is $15,491 a month, so if keeping Crux alive for those six segments costs more than that, cancelling dominates on direct economics. The decision also carried strategic inputs the break-even ignores.

Your totals do not foot. Which ARR is right?

Both, once you say what they are: the printed $185,892 is the six non-SV rows; all nine rows sum to $254,952, and the $69,060 difference is the three SV-bound segments. I lead with container shares because they foot to 294,581, and the appendix lists 77 names against 79 counted — I would confirm that before quoting a per-customer figure.

How many of the 55 will actually move?

The record does not say how many did. What I can show is the shape of the problem: 55 customers, $1,256 average ARR (69,060 / 55), 5 of them API integrations (9.1%) — a long tail suited to light-weight onboarding behind the pilot waves, with six already flagged churned across the whole book (7.6%).

Who disagreed, and what did they get right?

Four inputs: the cost of two systems and the stalled absorption project; a missing, unwritten parity list; "ejecting customers is never a good experience" and the timing of competitor cancellations; and cut-the-competitors. All four were right about something the decision then had to carry — the parity list is the one the record never closes.

How do you know the SMB path is cheap enough?

ARR per SMB customer is $769 against $46,504 per broker, a 60.5× spread — a bespoke migration for 39 accounts at that revenue does not pay, so the SMB path is the standard one. I would price the onboarding hours per account to confirm; there is no record figure.

What would you do on day one of a similar retirement?

Foot the table, find the concentration, write the rule per segment, put the dissent and the open questions on the same page, and date every action. Then price the keep-alive cost before the memo, not after.

The keep-alive cost is not in the record; the three values are illustrative bookends around the break-even. The strategic inputs — competitor timing, partner routes — are not in this arithmetic.

Total impact statement — say it like this

Honestly, retiring Crux was not a money-making decision and I don't dress it as one. The doc's printed total, about one hundred eighty-six thousand a year, is the revenue on the six segments that don't move to Shipment Visibility — I footed the table myself, and all nine rows sum to about two hundred fifty-five thousand. What the analysis changed was the shape of the work: three truck brokers were four percent of customers and seventy-five percent of the containers, so they got a partner route; fifty-five customers — the long tail — move through the pilot waves; nine are API-or-cancel, nine motor carriers cancel at wind-down, three were cancelled on dates. Every customer has a destination and a date. The record doesn't price keeping Crux alive, so I give the break-even — about fifteen and a half thousand a month — and I say the strategic inputs sit outside it. Every dollar here is the document's, footed, or labeled; none of it is claimed as savings.

If challenged — the full answers

"You are telling me a $186K book was a good decision to exit?"

I'm telling you what the record shows: the printed total is the six non-SV segments, and the rule exits or re-routes them by segment, with the brokers — 75% of containers — taking a partner route. Whether the economics beat the keep-alive cost is exactly the number the record never priced; I give the break-even, $15,491 a month, and flag it.

"Why trust your footing over the doc?"

Because the rows foot and the printed total does not: customers foot to 79, containers to 294,581, but the printed ARR equals the six non-transition rows only — the other three rows add $69,060. That is arithmetic, and it is why I use container shares first.

"What did you actually decide?"

The decider decided. My part, as I tell it, was the segmentation, the concentration read and getting the dissent and the open questions on one page beside the recommendation.

20-second version and NACF bridge
20-second version

When Flexport retired an acquired visibility product, 79 customers needed a destination. I segmented them into nine types, found three truck brokers were 4% of customers and 75% of containers, and wrote a rule per segment: 55 to the new product, three to a partner route, the rest API-or-cancel with dates — with the dissent on the page.

NACF bridge — one sentence

Retiring or re-pointing a node or lane in a network is the same problem: segment what flows through it, find the concentration (a few lanes carry most of the volume), give each segment a destination and a date, and price keeping both running — a placement trade-off of cost-to-serve against distance-to-customer.

Behavioral pack — four questions this story answers, each a separate 90-second STAR from a different facet

B1 · “Tell me about a time when you had to balance the needs of the customer with the needs of the business. What did you do? What was the result?” AIQB: Customer Obsession Q8 Customer Obsession facet: segmentation and destinations

Open (first two sentences): “Every one of seventy-nine customers got a destination, not an end-of-life email. I segmented them by what they actually were and wrote a rule per segment.”

90-second STAR. S An acquired product with 79 customers and a goal to move them by the end of 2022 — and not every customer fit. T Give each customer a path, not a notice. A Nine segments, three questions each — does SV serve them, partner or competitor, extra build — giving 55 to Shipment Visibility, three brokers to a partner route, nine API-or-cancel, nine cancel at wind-down and three cancelled on dates. R 79 of 79 with a destination and a date; the largest accounts got conversations instead of a template.

Land: 79 of 79: 55 + 3 + 9 + 9 + 3  ·  Pivot to NACF: retiring a lane or node: every flow through it gets a destination and a date before the old path closes.

Do NOT say: "nobody was stranded" or "zero escalations" — the record does not say; dollars as savings.

B2 · “Tell me about a time when you considered everyone who was affected by your work, not just your direct customers or people within your organization.” AIQB: Broad Responsibility Q5 Success and Scale Bring Broad Responsibility facet: concentration

Open (first two sentences): “Three customers were four percent of the book and seventy-five percent of the containers. A template would have been irresponsible for them and overkill for thirty-nine small accounts.”

90-second STAR. S A single end-of-life policy would have treated 79 customers alike. T Match the effort to what each customer needs and what is at stake. A I computed concentration: brokers 3.8% of customers, 75.0% of containers; ARR per customer from $769 (SMB) to $46,504 (broker), 60.5×. The brokers got a partner route and per-customer conversations; the tail got the standard path. R Effort followed stakes: partner route for the largest flows, standard path for the tail.

Land: 3.8% of customers = 75.0% of containers  ·  Pivot to NACF: a Pareto of lanes or nodes before a network change — the few that carry the volume get bespoke handling.

Do NOT say: that the partner route succeeded — the record shows the decision, not the outcome.

B3 · “Describe a time when you felt really strongly about something on a project but the team decided to go in a different direction. How hard did you press the issue? How did you approach that project afterward?” AIQB: Have a Backbone Q4 Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit facet: the dissent

Open (first two sentences): “The document carried real dissent: the cost of running two systems, a parity list nobody had written down, and a warning that ejecting customers is never a good experience. We put all of it on the page.”

90-second STAR. S A recommendation to retire a product that others had reasons to keep. T Get a decision the organization would commit to, with the objections visible. A The RAPID named who recommends, who agrees, who performs, who gives input and who decides; the input section kept each reviewer's words, including the parity and keep-alive questions; the decider committed with those on the page, and each impacted party confirmed they would perform. R A decision with named performers and dated actions — and two open questions flagged instead of hidden.

Land: four dissenting inputs; two open questions  ·  Pivot to NACF: a network decision memo — putting the cost of the status quo and the unresolved questions beside the recommendation, then committing.

Do NOT say: that I overruled anyone — the decider decided; or what my exact seat was without checking.

B4 · “Tell me about a time when you uncovered a significant problem in your team. What was it and how did you communicate it to your manager and other stakeholders? What did you do to address the problem?” AIQB: Earn Trust Q6 Dive Deep facet: footing

Open (first two sentences): “When I re-footed the decision document's table, the customer and container counts added up but the dollar total did not — the printed ARR was only six of the nine rows.”

90-second STAR. S A table everyone quoted: 79 customers, $185,892. T Make sure the numbers under a decision were right. A I summed each column from its rows: customers 79, containers 294,581, ARR $254,952 against the printed $185,892 — the difference, $69,060, is exactly the three SV-bound segments. I switched the analysis to container shares and flagged the 77-versus-79 name count. R The decision rested on numbers that foot; the dollar total was relabeled as the exit segments' ARR.

Land: $254,952 vs $185,892; difference $69,060  ·  Pivot to NACF: every network metric gets footed from its rows before a decision — totals that silently drop a segment are the commonest error.

Do NOT say: that I caught it live in the room — say "when I re-footed it preparing this".

Same story, three angles — three cards, three openers, three metrics, three LPs

Data / analytical angle Dive Deep

Opener: “Three customers were under four percent of the book and three-quarters of the containers — so the plan could not be one policy.”

Headline metric: 3.8% of customers = 75.0% of containers = 54.7% of ARR

  • Foot first: containers foot, the printed ARR does not.
  • Concentration: 3 brokers vs 39 SMBs — $46,504 vs $769 per customer.
  • The SV-bound 55 are the long tail: 69.6% of customers, 13.3% of containers.
  • Say "my footing" once, early.

Conflict / influence / backbone angle Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit

Opener: “The document carried real dissent, and the decision was only as strong as the objections left standing on the page.”

Headline metric: four dissenting inputs; one decider; each impacted party commits

  • Cost of two systems; a missing parity list; ejecting customers; cut the competitors.
  • RAPID: Recommend, Agree, Perform, Input, Decide named.
  • The decider committed with the objections recorded.
  • My part: the comparison, the per-segment pricing and the dissent on one page; the decider was an executive.

Honest failure-and-learning angle Learn and Be Curious

Opener: “The decision memo had two questions it could not answer — what Crux costs to keep running, and whether the feature-parity list was written down — and the record never closes either.”

Headline metric: 2 open questions; break-even $15,491 / month

  • What I would do differently: price the keep-alive cost before the memo, not after.
  • What I would add: a written parity matrix as an exit criterion for each segment.
  • What held: dated actions and named performers.
  • No dollar claimed as savings.
Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups

"You quote 79 customers and $185,892. Your own table says $254,952. Which is it?"

Numeric answer. Both, and I say what each is: $185,892 is the six non-transition rows (24 customers); the three SV-bound rows add $69,060 for $254,952. Customers (79) and containers (294,581) foot either way, so container shares lead. The appendix lists 77 names against 79 counted, so I would confirm before quoting a per-customer average.

Say exactly: “The printed total is the six segments that don't move to Shipment Visibility; all nine rows sum to about two hundred fifty-five thousand. Customers and containers foot either way, so I lead with container shares.”

"What if the three brokers refuse the partner route?"

Numeric answer. Then they cancel at wind-down per the decision, and the exposure is the largest in the book: 221,044 containers (75.0%) and $139,512 (54.7% of nine-row ARR). One of three already uses Transmission, which is why it is the route; the other two are per-customer conversations, not a template.

Say exactly: “If they decline, they cancel at wind-down — and that is seventy-five percent of the containers, which is exactly why they got per-customer conversations instead of a template.”

"What number would have flipped your decision to exit the forwarders and 3PLs?"

Numeric answer. The keep-alive cost. The nine customers pay $17,220 a year; the six exit segments together pay $15,491 a month. If keeping Crux alive costs less than that monthly figure, deferral wins on direct economics; the record never prices it, which is why I would price it before the memo.

Say exactly: “The keep-alive bill. The six exit segments pay fifteen thousand four hundred ninety-one a month; if running Crux costs less than that, deferral wins — and the record never prices it.”

S12 — Flexport — FF App export launch: re-sequencing a committed 18-month roadmap on cost-to-serve evidence (value in month 6, 66% less effort, ~$5M cost avoidance) DEEPENED

LPs this story can serve here: Think Big (primary — the flip) · Frugality (primary — ~$5M avoided by sequencing) · Have Backbone, Disagree & Commit (changing the manager's plan) · Earn Trust (privately first) · Bias for Action (the reversal trigger written before the swing)
Process family: Deliver — export documentation / operator and partner workflow; launch sequencing; unit-economics-driven roadmap governance
SAFE-SAY — two sentences to say exactly

Most attackable number. “About five million dollars of avoided cost — engineering effort never spent plus value pulled forward — said once, an avoidance and not a saving, and never added to the unit-cost arithmetic.”

“What's your source for that?” “It is the figure in my own interview workbook for the export-app re-plan; my split into engineering effort and value pulled forward comes to about four point four million, a model I label as mine, so I say about, and I never call it annual.”

Leadership Principles this story carries — one-line proof sentence each

Think Big The destination changed — first value in month 6 instead of month 18, and half again the unit saving — by re-opening a committed, socialized roadmap on evidence at the work-item grain.

Frugality About $5M of cost avoidance — engineering never spent plus value pulled forward — said once; 66% less effort to first value; a reuse rule for any module over 8 dev-weeks.

Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit I changed my manager's plan privately first, gave engineering two days to break the model, and the onsite ratified it with his name on it.

Earn Trust The cost of the trade — operators on the old screens two more quarters — was priced and published, not hidden.

Honesty label. The roadmap was my manager's; I owned the sequencing recommendation. Engineering produced the re-estimates; the ~$5M is the record's cost-avoidance figure, said once; the run-rate decomposition and the engineer-count split are my model. Loop-packet conflict (§1.3): that document tells this as a Sephora roadmap — the record is Flexport FF App; say Flexport.
What is the FF export app? — the one-minute explainer (from the program documents; record-from-docs)

The minute. The Freight Forwarding App — "FFA" — is Flexport's operator-facing execution platform: where import and export shipments actually get run. Its core tenet is work-item-driven execution — every unit of work is a work item assigned to exactly one operator ("one operator, one work item"), so nothing ships on tribal knowledge and every action is attributable. For export, the heart of it is schedule management: a "Generate Schedule" work item builds the shipment's milestone schedule automatically, and a "Replan Schedule" work item fires on defined exceptions — an LCL CFS-cutoff miss, a vessel late against truck pickup, a vessel late past the quoted delivery date, a change of vessel — each with a defined rework path instead of ad-hoc heroics. It shipped FCL first (2022), with Air and LCL behind it (2023). record-from-docs

The machinery behind the story. The schedule work sat inside a five-work-stream FFA program (Schedule Management; Cascading Schedules; Auto-Build Initial Schedule; End-to-End Operator Experience; Operator MiMeC), with schedule automation completing end Q3/early Q4 2022 and export-partner UAT aimed at a March 2023 launch. Because the app serves external partner organizations, it also carries a compliance bar: an immutable audit log of who changed what and when — nine event categories at a 100%-captured standard across ~100 partner orgs and thousands of changes a day, phased Alpha → Beta → MVP with CISO sign-off. record-from-docs

Doc-vs-canon footnotes: the module documents carry forward-plan dates (export-partner UAT March 2023; schedule automation completing end Q3/early Q4 2022) — the build-out clock after MVP. Canon's "value in month 6" is the sequencing decision's clock: different milestones, no conflict — never merge them. The audit-logs "~100 partner orgs" is platform-wide; canon's 53 is the import-partner migration set — never merge those either.

60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. The Freight Forwarding App export roadmap was operator-first — 18 months to first value, already socialized, with my manager's name on it. The process baselines were 339.5 minutes of handling per shipment and 2,200 operator interactions per departed shipment. The question nobody had asked was time-to-value and unit cost — the plan had been built around persona preference. Task. With no authority over the roadmap, re-sequence it on cost-to-serve evidence without abandoning the operators. Action. I wrote the baseline memo, had the business cut a bare-minimum scope per persona, and got engineering to re-estimate with confidence levels: operator-first reached −25% cost-to-serve in 9 months with operators still spending 20% of their time in the old app; partner-first reached −50% in 6 months and built the chassis operators would later ride. I added a rebuild-vs-reuse rule per module — over eight dev-weeks, reuse — a scope tripwire in the burn-up, and a written reversal trigger, then read it to my manager privately first; engineering had two days to break it, and the Bellevue onsite ratified the flip with his name on it. Operators' two further quarters on old screens were priced and published, not hidden. Result. Primary metric: engineering effort to first value — the 18-month plan → 66% less effort, value in month 6 instead of 18. ~$5M of cost avoidance — the record figure, said once; cost-to-serve −50% versus −25% at the work-item grain; 48 of 53 import partners migrated within a month.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — The inherited launch plan for the freight-forwarding app was operator-persona first, 18 months, already socialized, and my manager owned it. Process baselines: 339.5 minutes of handling per shipment and 2,200 operator interactions per departed shipment — the two numbers everything prices from.

The business problem was time-to-value and unit cost, not persona preference. Left as inherited, operators would reach first value in month 18 while still spending 20% of their time in the old app — half the unit saving (−25% vs −50% at the work-item grain), reached a year later. The risks of re-opening it: a committed, socialized plan; optimistic estimates; and operators feeling abandoned while partners went first.

T — Own the sequencing recommendation — and change a plan with my manager's name on it, without authority over the roadmap, in a way he could own.

Baselines on the table: 339.5 min/shipment · 2,200 operator interactions per departed shipment · first value in month 18 on the inherited path · cost-to-serve −25% (operator-first) vs −50% (partner-first) at the work-item grain.

Key challenges — what the program record adds (from the program documents; record-from-docs)
  • Asia latency surfaced right after the April 2022 import-side launch. The monthly business review read P95 page load at 21.19s in Asia against 3.3s in the US; static assets ran ~5s in China against ~500ms in the US; the app's domain had no geo-based DNS or reverse proxy, so Asia traffic rode the public internet. The fixes were sequenced — WAF enablement, a double CDN removed, a regional CDN proposal with smoke tests per change, then China and Asia tuning — with 48 of 53 import partners (about 90%) already migrated and expecting the app to hold. record-from-docs
  • Compliance was a launch feature, not an afterthought. The Audit Logs PRD demanded an immutable who/what/when trail across ~100 partner orgs and thousands of changes a day — nine categories at a 100%-captured bar, phased Alpha → Beta → MVP — and the CISO's definition of "important events" was deliberately vague, so PM buy-in and CISO sign-off were written into the plan as the mitigation. record-from-docs
  • Exceptions had to become work items. Replanning ran on a defined taxonomy — LCL CFS-cutoff miss, vessel late vs truck pickup, vessel late past quoted delivery date, change of vessel — each with a rework path, under the "one operator, one work item" tenet. The exception list, not the happy path, is where operator trust is won. record-from-docs
  • A hard external deadline. Export-partner launch UAT was set for March 2023, with schedule automation completing end Q3/early Q4 2022 and FCL ahead of Air/LCL — a date-driven constraint on top of the unit-economics sequencing. record-from-docs
Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery — baseline memo (339.5 min; 2,200 interactions); bare-minimum scope per persona cut with the business; the question restated as time-to-value and unit cost. Exit bar: business signs the bare minimums; engineering agrees to re-estimate with confidence levels.
  • Plan & Design — re-estimates 9 months (operator) vs 6 months (partner) with confidence buffers (×1.0 / ×1.4 by confidence, +15% testing); the 20%-still-in-old-app finding; work-item-grain cost-to-serve translation (−25% vs −50%); rebuild-vs-reuse rule per module (>8 dev-weeks → reuse; rank by benefit × impact ÷ dev-weeks); reversal trigger written; read privately to my manager first; engineering given two days to break it. Exit bar: my manager puts his name on the flip at the Bellevue onsite; sequencing signed.
  • Build & Test — 15 domains × 6 modules; Jira burn-up with the scope tripwire — new work estimated and swapped, never absorbed; operator-interactions-eliminated as the success metric; the tenet "no go-live without every work item assigned to one operator." Exit bar: burn-up 55.8% → 83.2% across two quarters; every work item owned.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (waves) — the import-side app live April 2022 with 11 partners in 10 countries; partner migration plan published; operators' two further quarters on old screens priced and published. Exit bar: 48 of 53 import partners migrated within a month.
  • Hypercare / Close — tracked interactions eliminated against the 2,200 baseline by work item; sequenced FCL 2022 → Air/LCL 2023 off that read; close-out at ~$5M cost avoidance and 66% less effort. Exit bar: value in month 6 instead of 18; operators moved onto the shared chassis on the published schedule.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the baseline memo, the comparison model, the rebuild/reuse rule, the scope tripwire and the reversal trigger. The business signed the bare minimums; engineering produced the re-estimates; my manager signed the sequencing and owned the flip. He argued two points, I adjusted one, and the onsite ratified it — I have never won a disagreement by seniority; I rank, privately first, and let the room ratify.

Designed the process; others own it now. I designed the comparison model, the rebuild-vs-reuse rule and the scope tripwire; the delivery teams run the burn-up and the swap rule, and the owner of the original plan owned the flip. Trade-off put to leadership. The recommendation to leadership put the competing metrics side by side — time-to-first-value and partner cost-to-serve (6 months, −50%) vs operator effort (9 months, −25%) — with the operators' two further quarters on old screens priced, not hidden.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality (canon numbers only)
  • $ — ~$5M cost avoidance (record, said once — never "avoidance" and "annual reduction" as two results); underneath it a ≈$1.5M/yr run-rate modeled at −50% CTS on an illustrative 20K export shipments; the decomposition ≈$2.9M engineering avoided + ≈$1.5M pulled forward ≈ $4.4M is my model, within ~12% of the record.
  • # — 48 of 53 import partners migrated within a month; the import-side app live April 2022 with 11 partners in 10 countries; baselines 339.5 min/shipment and 2,200 interactions (record).
  • % — engineering effort to first value −66%; CTS −50% partner-first vs −25% operator-first at the work-item grain; burn-up 55.8% → 83.2% across two quarters (record).
  • Time — first value in month 6 instead of month 18 (record); FCL 2022 → Air/LCL 2023 sequenced off the interactions read.
  • Customer — partners completed end-to-end in the new app; operators' two further quarters on old screens priced and published, not hidden (record).
  • Quality — new scope estimated and swapped, never absorbed; no go-live without every work item assigned to one operator (record).

Probing — challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer

HM — You changed your manager's plan. How, and would you do it to me?

Privately first, with the numbers — 9 versus 6 months, 20% still in the old app, −25% versus −50%. He argued two points, I adjusted one, and the onsite ratified it with his name on it. Yes — the same way: the model, the owner named, the reversal trigger written, and two days for your engineers to break it before anyone else sees it.

HM — Is the $5M a saving or an avoidance?

Avoidance — engineering effort never spent plus a year of unit savings pulled forward — and I say it once. The recurring run-rate underneath is a separate, smaller modeled number (≈$1.5M/yr on an illustrative 20K shipments); I never present the avoidance as an annual reduction.

HM — What stopped the operators from being abandoned?

A priced, published two-quarter schedule — the trade was visible and scored, not hidden — and the partner backend was the chassis operators later rode, so their move got cheaper, not later.

HM — Your engineer count and rate are yours, not the program's.

Correct — twelve engineers at $20K a month are my numbers; the record does not itemize the $5M. At 10–15 engineers and $15–25K the effort piece runs $1.8–4.5M; adding the pulled-forward run-rate (≈$1.5M at the base) puts the whole decomposition at $2.9–6.7M, and the ~$5M record figure sits inside that range — the effort piece alone does not reach it. I would firm it with the two scoping envelopes (66.2 and 99.8 engineer-months) and Finance's loaded rate.

HM — What made the big swing safe?

Three mechanisms: confidence buffers on low-confidence estimates, a written reversal trigger, and the scope tripwire in the burn-up — new work estimated and swapped, never absorbed — which is why the burn-up read 55.8 → 83.2% honestly.

HM — Lesson, and how it shows up in your first quarter here?

Bring the model, name the owner, write the reversal trigger. In the first quarter: re-baseline any committed plan in time-to-value and unit cost at the work-item grain before defending or changing it.

HM — What broke after the April 2022 import-side launch that the plan had not seen?

Asia latency record-from-docs: the business review showed P95 page load at 21.19 seconds in Asia against 3.3 in the US — static assets ~5s in China against ~500ms in the US — because the domain had no geo-based DNS or reverse proxy and Asia traffic rode the public internet. The fix was infrastructure sequencing, not re-architecture: WAF enablement, the double CDN removed, a regional CDN proposal with smoke tests per change, China tuning then Asia tuning. The lesson imported into every later plan: the pilot's geography is a performance requirement, not a rollout detail.

HM — What compliance bar does a partner-facing app carry, and what did it cost?

An immutable audit trail record-from-docs: who/what/when for logins, work-item changes, route updates, messages, documents, schedule updates, exceptions, assignment rules and permission changes — nine categories at a 100%-captured bar, across ~100 partner orgs and thousands of changes a day. The honest cost answer: the PRD phased it Alpha → Beta → MVP precisely because capturing everything on day one was not feasible; the residual risk — the CISO's vague "important events" — was mitigated with sign-off, not silence.

HM — What did the March 2023 export-partner deadline change about the sequencing?

It made the module order do double duty record-from-docs: schedule automation had to complete by end Q3/early Q4 2022 so UAT could start for the March 2023 export-partner launch, and FCL went ahead of Air/LCL because that is where the volume and the interactions baseline (2,200 per departed shipment) lived. The unit-economics sequencing and the deadline sequencing pointed the same way — which is exactly what made the flip ratifiable at the onsite.
