Where this lands at Amazon. Think Big with Frugality attached — re-open a committed plan when the goal matters more than the plan, price it in unit economics, and have the owner whose name was on the original ratify the change. For a NACF process roadmap: sequence by time-to-value and cost-to-serve at the work-item grain, with a reversal trigger and a scope tripwire written before the swing.

3 · Math & data logicFour tables: $152.78 ≈ $153; −25% vs −50%; 18 → 6 months; −66% ⇒ 34%; 27.6 vs 20 weeks; $2.85M + $1.52M ≈ $4.4M vs the ~$5M record.
4 · Implementation15 domains × 6 modules; burn-up 55.8% → 83.2%; import-side app live April 2022 with 11 partners in 10 countries; 48 of 53 import partners (about 90%) migrated; FCL 2022, Air and LCL 2023; the export launch is the later Q1 2023 milestone.
5 · Probe questionsSeven numeric probes plus three trap drills: avoidance vs saving, invented engineers, and the 27.6-week rule.

Honest note. The record's single engineering-effort figure is −66%; the effort-envelope numbers sometimes quoted alongside it are not used here.

Facet 5 — Probe questions (numeric)

Saving or avoidance? Annual?

Avoidance, one-time, said once: about $5M, record. My decomposition — $2.85M of engineering never spent plus $1.52M of unit saving pulled forward — is $4.4M, within 12% of it. The recurring run-rate underneath, $1.52M a year, is a separate modeled number; I never present the avoidance as annual.

Your engineers and rate are invented.

They are my model, and I say so: ten to fifteen engineers at $15–25K a month moves the effort piece from $1.8M to $4.5M; with the pulled-forward piece, the decomposition ranges $2.9M to $6.7M, and the record's ~$5M sits inside that range — not inside the effort piece alone.

Why 27.6 weeks and not 20?

(10 × 1.0 + 10 × 1.4) × 1.15 = 27.6: the 1.4 applies only to the low-confidence module and the 15% is testing buffer, so the plan is 38% longer than naive and honest about which parts are risky.

Why is partner-first twice the saving?

Because at the work-item grain operator-first reaches −25% in 9 months with 20% of operator time still in the old app, and partner-first reaches −50% in 6 months: $76 against $38 per shipment, 12 months earlier.

Why 8 dev-weeks?

It was engineering's median module cost — the record rule — so the threshold is theirs, not mine: over 8 dev-weeks, reuse; otherwise rebuild, ranked by benefit × impact ÷ dev-weeks.

What did operators pay?

Two more quarters on the old screens — priced, published and scored, not hidden. The partner backend was the chassis they later rode, so their move got cheaper, not later.

What broke after the April 2022 import-side launch?

Asia latency — the monthly review showed 21.19 seconds at P95 against 3.3 in the US; see S12-A for the full investigation. The lesson: the pilot's geography is a performance requirement, not a rollout detail.

Sensitivity — low / base / high
CaseEffort inputsEngineering avoided (one-time)Export shipmentsRun-rate / pulled forwardDecomposition total

Rows are modeled, not record; the program's ~$5M is the single signed figure and the base decomposition ($4.4M) is within 12% of it. Note: the high case here reads $6.7M on unrounded inputs (the master rounds to $6.8M).

Total impact statement — say it like this

About five million of cost avoidance, and I say that once. Roughly two point nine of it is engineering effort we never spent — sixty-six percent less on a plan that was eighteen months long — and about one and a half is a year of unit savings pulled forward because value landed in month six instead of month eighteen. Behind that sits a run-rate of about a million and a half a year at half the cost-to-serve on twenty thousand export shipments. The sixty-six percent, the six versus eighteen months and the minus fifty versus minus twenty-five are the record; the five million is the program's signed figure; the split and the shipment volume are my model.

If challenged — the full answers

"Twelve engineers and twenty thousand a month are yours, not the program's."

Correct, and the record does not itemize the five million, so I modeled the split. At ten to fifteen engineers and $15–25K a month the effort piece is $1.8M–$4.5M and the whole decomposition $2.9M–$6.7M; the record's ~$5M sits inside the decomposition range.

"Where do twenty thousand shipments come from?"

An illustrative FCL export volume, and the softest input: at 15,000 to 30,000 the run-rate runs $1.14M to $2.28M. I would replace it with the departed-shipment count from the system that produced the 2,200-interaction baseline.

"Are you double counting the pulled-forward value and the five million?"

No. The five million is the total; the pulled-forward year is one slice of how it decomposes, and the run-rate underneath is not a second result.

20-second version and NACF bridge
20-second version

At Flexport I re-sequenced a committed 18-month roadmap on cost-to-serve evidence — partners first, operators on the same chassis later — so value landed in month 6, effort to first value fell 66%, and the program recorded about $5M of cost avoidance.

NACF bridge — one sentence

It is the discipline of sequencing launches or placement moves by time-to-value at the unit grain, with a written reversal trigger and the owner of the original plan ratifying the change.

Behavioral pack — four questions this story answers, each a separate 90-second STAR from a different facet

B1 · “Give me an example of how you have changed the direction or view of a specific function/department and helped them embrace a new way of thinking. Why was a change needed? What was the outcome?” AIQB: Think Big Q2 Think Big facet: solution design

Open (first two sentences): “The roadmap said eighteen months to first value, operators first. I re-opened it, and the new plan put value in month six and delivered twice the unit saving.”

90-second STAR. S A committed, socialized 18-month plan owned by my manager. T Change the plan without authority over the roadmap, in a way he could own. A I priced both paths at the work-item grain — partner-first 6 months and −50%, operator-first 9 months and −25% with 20% of operator time still in the old app — added confidence buffers, a rebuild-vs-reuse rule, a scope tripwire and a written reversal trigger. R First value in month 6 instead of 18; effort to first value −66%; ~$5M cost avoidance, said once.

Land: 18 → 6 months; −66% effort  ·  Pivot to NACF: sequencing launches by time-to-value at the unit grain.

Do NOT say: "$5M annual savings" or the roadmap as mine.

B2 · “Tell me about a time when you strongly disagreed with your manager or peer on something you considered very important to the business. What was it and how did you handle it? Knowing what you know now, would you do anything differently?” AIQB: Have a Backbone Q1 Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit facet: implementation

Open (first two sentences): “The plan had my manager's name on it. I read it to him privately first, with the numbers, and gave engineering two days to break my model before anyone else saw it.”

90-second STAR. S A plan my manager owned, with socialized commitments. T Change it without becoming a political fight. A Private read; he argued two points and I adjusted one; engineering got two days to break the model; the onsite ratified with his name on the flip. R He owned the flip; the plan changed; the model survived.

Land: two points argued, one adjusted  ·  Pivot to NACF: changing a launch sequence: model first, private first, owner of the old plan on the new one.

Do NOT say: that I won on seniority or escalated — I never have.

B3 · “Give me an example of how you have helped save costs or eliminate waste within your role or organization. What was the situation? What was the impact?” AIQB: Frugality Q1 Frugality facet: math and data logic

Open (first two sentences): “About five million dollars of cost avoidance — engineering we never spent plus a year of unit savings pulled forward — and I say it once, because it is avoidance, not an annual reduction.”

90-second STAR. S An 18-month plan that would have consumed the full engineering effort. T Reach first value with less spend. A Reuse over 8 dev-weeks, partner backend as the shared chassis, confidence buffers, scope swaps instead of absorption. R Effort −66%; decomposition $2.85M + $1.52M = $4.4M against the record ~$5M.

Land: ~$5M cost avoidance (record), said once  ·  Pivot to NACF: reuse over rebuild in a network program: the 8-week rule is an engineering-median threshold.

Do NOT say: avoidance and annual reduction as two results; the engineers and rate as the program's.

B4 · “Tell me about a time when you had to communicate a change in direction that you anticipated people would have concerns with. What did you do to understand the concerns and mitigate them?” AIQB: Earn Trust Q1 Earn Trust facet: data analysis

Open (first two sentences): “Operators would stay on the old screens two more quarters. I priced that and published it instead of hiding it.”

90-second STAR. S A sequencing choice that delayed one group for the benefit of the whole. T Be transparent about who pays. A The baseline memo showed 20% of operator time still in the old app under the inherited path; I priced two more quarters for operators, published the schedule, and made the partner backend the chassis they later ride. R No hidden trade; operators' move got cheaper, not later.

Land: two quarters, priced and published  ·  Pivot to NACF: a network change that burdens one node temporarily: price it, publish it, give the date it ends.

Do NOT say: that the delay was costless.

Same story, three angles — three cards, three openers, three metrics, three LPs

Data / analytical angle Dive Deep

Opener: “Three hundred thirty-nine and a half minutes and twenty-two hundred interactions per shipment — with those two numbers the roadmap debate became arithmetic.”

Headline metric: 339.5 min × $0.45 ≈ $153; −25% vs −50%

  • Price each path at the work-item grain.
  • The 20% residue caps operator-first at half.
  • Confidence buffers: 27.6 vs 20 weeks.
  • Say "derived" on the $153.

Conflict / influence / backbone angle Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit

Opener: “It was my manager's plan. I read it to him alone, with numbers, and gave engineering two days to break it.”

Headline metric: two points argued; one adjusted

  • Private first, then the room.
  • His name on the flip.
  • Reversal trigger written in advance.
  • Never won on seniority.

Honest failure-and-learning angle Are Right, A Lot

Opener: “What I did not see was Asia: right after the April 2022 import-side launch, the review showed 21 seconds at P95 against 3.3 in the US.”

Headline metric: 21.19 s vs 3.3 s

  • The plan sequenced by value and effort, not by the pilot's geography.
  • The fix was infrastructure sequencing (see S12-A).
  • The lesson imported into later plans: geography is a performance requirement.
  • What held: the sequencing decision itself.
Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups

"Five million — is that saved every year?"

Numeric answer. No. It is one-time avoidance, the record's signed figure. My decomposition is $2.85M engineering never spent + $1.52M pulled forward = $4.4M; the recurring run-rate is $1.52M a year, a separate modeled number on illustrative volume. They are never both claimed.

Say exactly: “It's avoidance — one time, said once. The run-rate underneath is a separate, smaller model; I never present the avoidance as annual.”

"Your twelve engineers — where did they come from?"

Numeric answer. My model: 12 × 18 months × 66% × $20,000 = $2,851,200. At 10–15 engineers and $15–25K the effort piece is $1.8M–$4.5M; with the pulled-forward piece the decomposition is $2.9M–$6.7M, and the record's ~$5M sits inside that range.

Say exactly: “Those are my modeled inputs — the record doesn't itemize the five million. Across ten to fifteen engineers it moves from about two to four and a half million; I'd firm it with Finance's rate.”

"Why is your plan 27.6 weeks when two 10-week modules is 20?"

Numeric answer. (10 × 1.0 + 10 × 1.4) × 1.15 = 27.6 — the 1.4 applies only to the low-confidence module, plus a 15% testing buffer; 38% over naive, and the buffer is not spread evenly.

Say exactly: “The buffer only goes where confidence is low — twenty-seven point six against twenty naive — so the extra eight weeks are the risky module, not padding.”

S12-A — Flexport — Why Asia waited 21 seconds: a P95 latency investigation that split one complaint into a static-content problem and a routing problem, then ran it as a dated, smoke-tested change plan NEW

LPs this story can serve here: Dive Deep (primary) · Customer Obsession · Bias for Action · Insist on the Highest Standards
Process family: Improve — root-cause investigation of a regional performance gap; change control for network and security changes
Leadership Principles this story carries — one-line proof sentence each

Dive Deep The review said "Asia is slow"; the work was to find where the 21.19 seconds went — region split, static versus dynamic, and a China static-JS P95 of 5 s against 0.5 s in the US — before anyone bought a CDN.

Customer Obsession Asia was 45.3% of the 13.92 thousand page views in the window, so the slowest region was the largest region — the plan was ordered by where the partners actually were.

Bias for Action Reversible fixes went first — double CDN removed in China, WAF on globally — and the expensive one (CDN for the dynamic host) was deferred until the metrics said it was needed.

Insist on the Highest Standards Every WAF or CDN change ran a written pre-check, a post-check and a rollback-able DNS override — 32 smoke checks a change — and I will not claim a single-cause before-and-after that the record does not support.

Honesty label. This is an investigation story with a split of credit: the investigation document is the app team engineer's, with the network infrastructure owner running the CDN and WAF changes — I did not write the root-cause doc or make the network changes. What I tell as mine is the program side: the monthly review that surfaced the gap, the cut by region, the dated plan with owners, the change-control discipline and the insistence on a like-for-like measurement. My seat (my own workbook, Apple v11 B3, and the document): the investigation is authored by the app engineer, and the network-infrastructure owner made the CDN and DNS changes (the plan's owners are named in the document); I drove the follow-ups from the monthly review and kept the plan, the sequencing and the China-host smoke tests honest. I say "I drove the follow-ups", not "I fixed the CDN". Before-and-after flag: the investigation window (Apr 24 – May 10) and the review series differ in window and region definition, so the 81% figure I can compute is indicative only — I do not quote it as the result; the like-for-like record is the review series. The document's Asia table and its China-specific plan rows belong to one investigation, with China the sharpest tail; the later review series splits Asia (non-China) from China, so the 21.19 s and the later 4.0 s may not be the same population — I never subtract across them. Overlap: this story is also on the bench in another interviewer's packet.
What the record says (from the investigation document and the weekly review sheet; record-from-docs)

Context. The Freight Forwarding App (FFA) launched its import-side app in April 2022 to 11 partners across 10 countries; through May, 48 of 53 import partners were migrated. The monthly review showed Asia countries with much higher latency than other regions. record-from-docs

The numbers. Page-load P95 for Apr 24 – May 10 (Datadog view loading time, which covers static resources, the GraphQL request and the render): US 3.3 s on 2.19k views; Asia 21.19 s on 6.31k views; Others 4.45 s on 5.42k views. Static JavaScript P95: China 5 s against 500 ms in the US. record-from-docs

Two named causes. (1) Static resources are served from a CDN host that performs poorly in China; (2) the dynamic host has no geo-based DNS or regional reverse proxy for Asia, so GraphQL requests cross the public internet instead of the data-center backbone. record-from-docs

The plan. Eight rows, owners and ETAs: three security (WAF proposal, WAF for China, WAF globally — follow-ups to an incident) and five latency (remove the double CDN in China — done, ETA June 24; static-CDN proposal for China and Asia, July 15; tuning for China, July 30; tuning for Asia, August 15; CDN for the dynamic host globally — "decide based on the metrics", Q3 or later). A written change process: override DNS locally, verify resolution, smoke-test internal single-sign-on and partner users, repeat on the China instance, change, then remove the override and re-test. record-from-docs

The review series. The weekly review sheet carries page-load P95 overall and by region (weeks 33 to 52 and week 1) and a monthly view (April to September). The sheet's action items include adding GraphQL latency at P50, P90 and P95 by region (done October 11, 2022). record-from-docs

60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. We had launched the import-side freight-forwarder app in April 2022 and moved 48 of 53 import partners onto it by May. At the monthly review the latency chart had an outlier: Asia. Task. Find out whether it was one problem or several, and where the time was going, before anyone bought anything. Action. I cut the 13.92 thousand views by region over the 17-day window: Asia was 45% of the views and waited 21.19 seconds at the 95th percentile against 3.3 in the US — 6.4 times. Then I asked where the time goes: static JavaScript took 5 seconds in China against half a second in the US, and the dynamic requests from Asia went over the public internet because there was no regional edge. Two causes, two owners. I ordered the plan by reversibility — remove the double CDN, turn on the firewall — held the expensive global CDN until the metrics asked for it, and put every network change behind a written DNS-override and smoke-test checklist. Result. Primary metric: the Asia-to-US gap of 6.42× — and in the review series that followed, Asia read 4.0 seconds against 4.09 in North America by the last week of the year, with overall monthly P95 down from 8.39 to 4.68 seconds. I tell that as a gap that closed, not as a single-cause saving.

SAFE-SAY — two sentences to say exactly

Most attackable number. “Asia waited about twenty-one seconds at the ninety-fifth percentile against about three and a third in the US — roughly six and a half times — over a seventeen-day window of about fourteen thousand page views.”

“What's your source for that?” “It is the latency document's own table; the six-point-four is my division of its two printed figures, and the later four seconds against four-oh-nine is from the monthly review series — I describe it as a gap that closed, not a single-cause saving.”

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — Flexport's freight-forwarder app was in its first months: the import-side app live to 11 partners in 10 countries in April 2022, then 48 of 53 import partners (about 90%) by the end of May. The monthly review flagged that Asian users saw far higher page-load latency than anyone else — 21.19 seconds at P95 against 3.3 in the US on the same two-and-a-half-week window — and Asia was the single largest slice of traffic. The risk: a freight partner who waits twenty seconds for a shipment page goes back to email, and the cost-to-serve case for the whole migration depends on partners using the app.

T — Turn "Asia is slow" into named causes with owners and a dated plan, sequence the changes so the cheap and reversible ones run first, keep the production network and security changes safe, and prove the improvement with a measurement that can be trusted.

Baselines on the table: Page-load P95: US 3.3 s · Asia 21.19 s · Others 4.45 s · views 2.19k / 6.31k / 5.42k · window Apr 24 – May 10 (17 days) · static JS P95 China 5 s vs US 0.5 s · 48 of 53 partners migrated · 8 plan rows (3 security, 5 latency).

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery — cut P95 and views by region over the window; read the resource-level data; mapped the request path for static content and for GraphQL; wrote the two-cause hypothesis and what each predicts. Exit bar: a region table with views, and a named hypothesis per cause.
  • Plan & Design — sequenced eight rows by reversibility and dependency: security first where an incident was open, then the double-CDN removal, then proposals, then tuning; wrote the decision rule to defer the global CDN until the metrics asked for it; listed the open measurement question — how to test the China CDN in a consistent, reproducible way. Exit bar: owners and ETAs on every row; one conditional row with its trigger.
  • Build & Test — the change process: override DNS locally, verify resolution, smoke-test internal single-sign-on and partner log-in, workview, log-out and re-log-in, repeat on the China instance; infra performs the change; then remove the override, clear the cache and test again. Exit bar: 32 checks per change (16 before, 16 after).
  • Cutover & Go-Live — WAF on for China and globally and the double CDN removed in China, each behind the checklist, ETAs June 24 – June 30; static-asset tuning for China and Asia dated July 30 and August 15. Exit bar: four of eight rows marked done in the document; dated owners on the rest.
  • Hypercare / Close — the monthly and weekly reviews carry page-load P95 by region; the action item to add P50 / P90 / P95 GraphQL latency by region closed October 11, 2022; the week-47 spike was triaged against the region split rather than assumed to be the network. Exit bar: a regional series the review reads every week.

Influence and ownership. Mine (as I would tell it): the review that surfaced the gap, the region cut, the sequencing rule, the change checklist discipline and the insistence on a like-for-like measure. Not mine: the root-cause write-up, the CDN and WAF changes, the security sign-offs and the app optimizations — the app engineer and the network-infra owner own those, with the security reviewer signing the firewall proposal.

Designed the process; others own it now. The change process, the region cut in the weekly review and the P50 / P90 / P95 action item live in the document and the sheet, not in my head. Trade-off put to leadership. Buy a global CDN now (certain cost, unknown benefit) against two cheaper reversible fixes first and a metrics trigger — the schedule risk was that the global CDN row slipped to Q3 or later.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality (record vs modeled labeled)
  • $ — No dollar exists in the record. I model exposure, labeled illustrative: Asia's 45.3% of views against the S12 run-rate ≈ $0.69M a year (modeled upper bound, not savings); the direct tail time is ≈ $1.6K a year, which is why the case is adoption, not seconds.
  • # — Asia 21.19 s vs US 3.3 s = 6.42× on 6.31k of 13.92k views; 32 smoke checks per change; 8 plan rows, 4 marked done.
  • % — overall page-load P95, review monthly series, April to September: 8.39 s → 4.68 s, −44.2% (record-from-docs); Asia (non-China) 5.5 → 4.0 s, −27.3%, North America 5.62 → 4.09 s, −27.2% over weeks 39 to 52 (record-from-docs, my arithmetic).
  • Time — plan span June 24 → August 15 = 52 days; the P50 / P90 / P95 by-region action item closed October 11, 2022.
  • Customer — Asia partners were the largest slice of views; by the last week of the year the review series put Asia (non-China) within 2% of North America, and China at 1.57× North America.
  • Quality — zero unreviewed network changes — every WAF or CDN change had its pre-check and post-check — and a before-and-after claim limited to what the like-for-like series supports.
L6 level evidence — this story against the Amazon L6 functional rubric; every line is this story's record or labeled model
L6 dimensionEvidence in this story
AmbiguityOne complaint, no known cause. The work was to split it into two testable causes (static content path, dynamic routing path) and to name what would disprove each.
Scope & influenceEngineering, network infrastructure and security each owned a piece; the plan was a one-page sequence with owners and dates that none of them could do alone.
Execution trade-off + mechanism others now ownDeferred the global CDN on a metrics trigger — a deliberate schedule risk. The mechanism others own: the change checklist, the by-region series and the P50 / P90 / P95 view.
Impact (long-term)The review series that followed shows the Asia gap gone by week 39 and China as the remaining outlier — a clear next investigation instead of a vague complaint.
Process improvement (standard work institutionalized)DNS-override + smoke-test + rollback as the standard for WAF and CDN changes; regional percentiles on the weekly review.
Complexity & consensusApp team, network infrastructure and security with different incentives — a written plan with a conditional row resolved the "just buy the CDN" push without a standoff.
Root cause at scaleThe 6.42× gap was a path problem, not an app problem — week 47 proved it from the other side, when North America spiked 2.09× as well.
Five facets at a glance — the labeled structure the probing section below follows
1 · Data analysisP95 and views by region; Asia 45.3% of views; static JS 10× slower in China; P95 versus P99 sample sizes (63 observations against 22); the review series by region and week.
2 · Solution designStatic path versus dynamic path; six options weighed; reversible-first sequence; metrics trigger for the global CDN.
3 · Math & data logicGap ratios 6.42× and 1.35×; tail views 315.5; 52-day plan; 32 checks; −44.2% overall; Asia / NA 0.98×; exposure $0.69M (illustrative).
4 · ImplementationEight plan rows with owners and ETAs; the DNS-override change process; WAF first, double CDN removed next; China tuning, then Asia.
5 · Probe questionsSeven numeric probes plus three trap drills: the 81% temptation, P95s that do not add, P99 on thin data.
Probing — five facets, then the full drill

Facet 1 — Data analysis (what data, grain, joins, cuts, what it revealed)

What I read. Three sources, all record-from-docs: (1) the investigation document's region table — page-load P95 and views for Apr 24 – May 10; (2) its resource-level cut — static JS P95 China 5 s vs US 0.5 s; (3) the weekly and monthly review sheet by region (weeks 33–52, week 1; April–September monthly). I added the shares, the ratios and the tail-sample arithmetic.

RegionPage-load P95Views% of viewsvs USTail views (5%)
US3.30 s2,19015.7%1.00×109.5
Asia21.19 s6,31045.3%6.42×315.5
Others4.45 s5,42038.9%1.35×271.0
Total—13,920100%—696

What it revealed. (1) The largest region was the slowest: Asia carried 45.3% of views at 6.42× the US P95. (2) Two paths, two causes: static JS in China at 10× the US P95; dynamic GraphQL from Asia over the public internet with no regional edge. (3) Why P95 and not P99: in 17 days Asia has 6,310 views, so P99 rests on 63 observations and the US P99 on 22 — P95 rests on 316 and 110, which is stable enough to steer by. (4) P95s do not add: the 5 s static-JS P95 is not 5 s of the 21.19 s page-load P95; the right test is to re-measure page load after every change. (5) The later review series is a different population — it splits Asia (non-China) from China — so I never subtract across the two sources. (6) Week 47: North America went from 4.52 to 9.44 s (2.09×) in the same week Asia went from 7.16 to 16.4 s (2.29×) while China fell — an application-wide regression, not a regional network one. The review sheet's own 12/12 callout explains a GraphQL-latency spike for the Core and Client Apps as a change in the metrics code that made the dashboard numbers wrong, fixed retroactively — a measurement error, not a network one; the October regression is a separate cause (work_view 2.0 un-optimized queries, action item 11). I do not quote week 47 as an Asia result.

Change process (record) and my controls. Before: local DNS override, verify resolution, smoke test for internal and partner users, repeat on the China instance. Change: network infra only. After: remove the override on both hosts, clear the cache, re-test. Going to see where the seconds are is genchi genbutsu; the two causes are root-cause analysis (5 Whys by path); the DNS-override checklist is poka-yoke and standard work; each change followed by a re-measure is PDCA; removing the double CDN is eliminating a waste step; every tail view is waiting waste.

Facet 5 — Probe questions (hardest follow-ups, numeric answers)

Why P95 and not the average or P99?

The average hides the tail users feel and P99 is too thin: in 17 days Asia has 6,310 views, so P99 rests on 63 observations and the US on 22, while P95 rests on 316 and 110. P95 is stable enough to steer by and sensitive enough to show 21.19 seconds against 3.3.

How do you know it was a network problem and not the app?

Two checks. Resource-level: static JS P95 was 5 s in China against 0.5 s in the US. And by region over time: in week 47 North America doubled (4.52 → 9.44, 2.09×) with Asia (7.16 → 16.4, 2.29×) while China fell — that is an application regression, a different problem, which I did not blame on the network.

Did the plan actually close the gap?

The like-for-like review series says the Asia (non-China) gap to North America was closed by week 39 — 5.5 against 5.62 — and stayed there at week 52 (4.0 against 4.09); China remained at 1.57×. What I cannot show is which change moved it between May and September — the record does not attribute.

Why defer the global CDN?

Because two reversible changes were ahead of it and the document itself said to decide on the metrics. A global CDN is a certain cost and an unknown benefit; the right time to buy it is when the regional P95 stays above the others after tuning.

What did the investigation cost and save in dollars?

The record prices nothing. I model it as adoption protection: Asia was 45.3% of views, and 45.3% of the S12 run-rate is ≈ $0.69M a year of exposure — an upper bound, not savings. The direct tail time is about $1.6K a year, which is why the argument was never seconds times dollars.

What would you do differently?

Start with P50, P90 and P95 by region from day one — the action item to add them closed only on October 11, 2022 — and decide up front how to benchmark the China CDN consistently, which the document listed as an open question.

Your 81% — where is it from?

It is (21.19 − 4.0) / 21.19, which mixes the 17-day investigation window with a week-52 review figure — an indicative number, not a result. The like-for-like numbers are −27.3% for Asia over weeks 39 to 52 and −44.2% overall from April to September.

Business impact model — KPI tree
Asia partners can use the app without waiting: gap to the home region closed, tail visible, changes safe
+- Gap: Asia 21.19 s vs US 3.30 s = 6.42x on 45.3% of views (record)
|    +- static path: China JS 5 s vs 0.5 s = 10x -> remove double CDN (done) -> proposal -> tuning
|    +- dynamic path: no geo DNS / regional proxy -> GraphQL over the public internet -> edge proxy (CDN deferred on metrics)
+- Result (review series, record): overall P95 8.39 -> 4.68 s (-44.2%); Asia 5.5 -> 4.0 s vs NA 5.62 -> 4.09 s; China 6.43 s = 1.57x NA
+- Control: 32 smoke checks per WAF / CDN change; P50 / P90 / P95 by region on the weekly review
+- $ (illustrative / modeled): tail time ~ $1.6K/yr; exposure ~ $0.69M/yr (upper bound, not savings)
(loop)  complaint -> split by region -> split by path -> reversible fix first -> re-measure -> next fix
Solution flow
monthly review: Asia is the outlier
   -> cut by region: views, P95  (17 days; Asia 45.3% of views, 6.42x US)
   -> cut by path: static (CDN) vs dynamic (GraphQL route)
   -> two causes, two owners, one dated plan (8 rows)
   -> every change: DNS override -> smoke test x16 -> change -> remove override -> smoke test x16
   -> re-read regional P95 -> decide: next tuning row, or the conditional CDN row
Sensitivity — low / base / high

Total impact statement — say it like this

The record number here is a gap, not a dollar. At the monthly review Asia showed a twenty-one second page-load P95 against three-point-three in the US — six and a half times — on forty-five percent of the views. I split it into two paths: static content in China, where the JavaScript took five seconds against half a second, and dynamic requests that crossed the public internet with no regional edge. We sequenced the cheap, reversible changes first, put every network change behind a thirty-two-check smoke test, and held the expensive global CDN until the metrics asked for it. In the review series that followed, overall monthly P95 fell from eight-point-four to four-point-seven seconds, and Asia read four seconds against four-point-one in North America by the last week of the year. I tell that as a gap that closed, not as one change that saved something — the record doesn't attribute it, and any dollars I attach, about seven-tenths of a million of exposure, are modeled and not claimed.

If challenged — the full answers

"You are claiming credit for an infrastructure fix you didn't build."

No. The root-cause document and the CDN and WAF changes were the app engineer's and the network-infra owner's. What I own is the region cut, the sequence, the change checklist discipline and the measurement honesty — and I say so in the first sentence.

"Latency is a technical metric. Where is the business?"

Asia was 45.3% of views, and the cost-to-serve case for the whole migration depends on partners using the app. The exposure on the S12 run-rate is ≈ $0.69M a year as a modeled upper bound; the direct tail time is ≈ $1.6K — which is the point: the case is adoption.

"Why not just throw a global CDN at it?"

Because the reversible items came first and the document tied the global CDN to the metrics. A CDN would add cost and ops without telling us which hop is slow.

20-second version and NACF bridge
20-second version

At Flexport the monthly review showed Asian users waiting 21 seconds at P95 on the freight-forwarder app — 6.4 times the US, on 45% of the views. I split it into a static-content problem and a routing problem, sequenced the reversible fixes first behind a 32-check smoke test, and the review series later showed Asia level with North America.

NACF bridge — one sentence

A geo-routed edge near the user with a backbone between sites is the cross-dock placement idea in software: put a node near demand, run the linehaul between hubs, and measure the tail — a P95 on inbound transit or dock wait, not the average, is what sizes appointment buffers and capacity.

Data / analytical angle Dive Deep

  • Sample-size logic: P99 on 63 observations vs 22 in the US; P95 on 316 / 110.

  • Week 47: NA 2.09×, Asia 2.29× — application, not network.

Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups

"So you cut Asia latency by 81%."

Numeric answer. No. (21.19 − 4.0) / 21.19 = 81.1% subtracts a 17-day investigation figure from a week-52 review figure that may not be the same region population. Like-for-like: Asia (non-China) 5.5 → 4.0 s = −27.3% over weeks 39 to 52; overall monthly 8.39 → 4.68 s = −44.2%.

Say exactly: “I wouldn't quote eighty-one percent — it compares two different windows. The like-for-like review series shows overall P95 down forty-four percent from April to September, and Asia level with North America by week 39.”

"Static JS was 5 seconds of your 21 — so fixing it saves 5 seconds?"

Numeric answer. No. A P95 of one resource is not a slice of a page-load P95, and P95s do not add. The 5 s China JS P95 against 0.5 s in the US (10×) points at the path; the number to steer by is the page-load P95, re-measured after each change.

Say exactly: “Percentiles don't add — five seconds at P95 for one resource isn't five seconds off the page. I re-measured page-load P95 after each change instead.”

"Why P95? Why not P99?"

Numeric answer. Sample size. In the 17-day window Asia had 6,310 views, so P99 rests on 1% × 6,310 = 63.1 observations and the US on 21.9; P95 rests on 315.5 and 109.5. At roughly 2,600 Asia views a week, a weekly P95 still has about 130 tail views.

Say exactly: “In seventeen days the US had about twenty-two views past its P99 — too thin to steer by. P95 had over a hundred in the US and over three hundred in Asia.”

S16-A — Flexport — The data model under the Monday review: a container-week grain, a ticket-to-container bridge, a 1% parity gate and a sync-drop alarm that turned four hand-stitched systems into one Snowflake pipeline NEW

LPs this story can serve here: Dive Deep (primary) · Invent and Simplify · Frugality · Insist on the Highest Standards
Process family: Improve — data pipeline and reconciliation design under an operating metric; build-vs-bridge and cutover gating
Leadership Principles this story carries — one-line proof sentence each

Dive Deep The cost-to-serve ratio only works if tickets and containers are counted at the same grain; a join at the wrong grain would have made it look 44.4% better than it was (illustrative), so the grain was fixed before the pipeline.

Invent and Simplify A ticket-to-container cross-reference table shipped the join now, while the real key was pushed upstream to Support — ship with the bridge, fix the source — and seven frozen metrics replaced a pile of ad-hoc pulls.

Frugality The status quo was priced in the BRD (over $100K a year, priced not saved) so the infrastructure team could prioritize the build themselves; scope stayed at seven metrics and the existing BI tool — no new system bought.

Insist on the Highest Standards Nothing switched until every frozen metric matched the manual pull within 1% for consecutive weeks, every mismatch explained by cause, and a failed feed raised an alert instead of silence.

Honesty label. I wrote the business requirements document and owned the acceptance gate, the parallel-run procedure and the cutover call; the infrastructure team built the pipeline — I did not lay pipe, I made the pipe buildable (Apple A3). The ">$100K a year" is a priced baseline — priced, not claimed as savings: the BRD's own arithmetic is $103,680 (30 → 6 hours a week across two weekly reviews at an assumed $90 an hour) and my split of it into hours and decision risk is a model. The BRD names no table or column — it names the Zendesk Ticket ID, the Crux Container Number, Flexport core data and Looker SQL Runner views — so I describe the join, the grain and the artifacts, not a production schema; the container-week grain is my design logic, anchored to the weekly-stock grain the review already used (Apple A2). Four systems: the BRD names the ticket system, the Crux container pull (by hand, in Google Sheets) and core data; Apple A3's whiteboard lists ticketing, scraper registry, product-event DB and CRM — say "four systems" and name the ticket system and the container pull with confidence. Overlap: the failure angle here is the data-access security review (Apple G3), told as the one miss it was; the data-model, grain and reconciliation angle is this story's own.
What the record says (from the business requirements document and the program notes; record-from-docs)

Problem. Operator intervention for Shipment Visibility is managed in the ticketing system: a ticket is created when the visibility service fails to start tracking a submitted container automatically, and Data Services closes it. To track key metrics automatically, ticket data must be joined to container numbers from the visibility vendor's data, and then to core shipment data. record-from-docs

As-is. Manual pulls from the ticketing system matched to a manual pull of containers into spreadsheets: 1.5–2 hours on average to produce one week-over-week snapshot of seven metrics (coverage automated, coverage with human input, tickets generated, tickets per active container, 4PL % within SLA, 3PL % within SLA, and the 4PL-vs-3PL delta). record-from-docs

Requirements. BR-1 customer data protected; BR-2 interoperates with other capabilities; BR-3 feed daily, preferably hourly; BR-4 an alert to internal support users when the feed fails. Objectives: automated reporting through the BI tool's SQL runner; easy joins to core data. record-from-docs

The pricing in the document. "On Average 3 resources are spending 5 hrs per week for 2 WBR's" at an assumed $90 an hour — printed as a $10,880 monthly cost amounting to $129,600 a year; reduced to 1 hr a week, $25,920; "Savings here would be $103,680 a year" (BRD p1–2; 30 → 6 hours a week). The printed monthly figure does not match its own annual (3 × 5 × 2 × 4 × $90 = $10,800). record-from-docs Say: "over $100K a year — the BRD priced it at $103,680 on thirty hours a week at an assumed ninety dollars an hour; my split into hours and decision risk is a model; priced, not a claimed saving."

60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. The Monday review ran on four systems that did not talk, stitched together in spreadsheets by me — system accuracy 60%, 1.5 to 2 hours a snapshot, and tickets that carried no container number, so the program's cost-to-serve could not be computed from the data. Task. Write the requirements and the acceptance test for a pipeline that produces the same seven numbers without me. Action. I fixed the grain first — container by week, with tickets and containers counted at the same grain, because a join at ticket grain would have made cost-to-serve look about 44% better than it was. I shipped a ticket-to-container cross-reference table so the join could go live and pushed a real container ID upstream to Support. I priced the status quo in the BRD so the infrastructure team could prioritize the build, then set the gate: every frozen metric within 1% of the manual pull for consecutive weeks, mismatches explained by cause — mostly late tickets on the manual side — and an alert whenever the feed fails. Result. Primary metric: system accuracy 60% to 90% on one pipeline instead of four systems — and the first automated catch was a ticket-sync drop of about 4% that nobody could have seen by hand. The dollar I carry is a price in the BRD — over $100K a year, $103,680 on its own arithmetic — priced, not a saving.

SAFE-SAY — two sentences to say exactly

Most attackable number. “The review was priced at over a hundred thousand dollars a year — the requirements document works it to a hundred and three thousand, six hundred and eighty on thirty hours a week at an assumed ninety dollars an hour — and that is a price, not a saving I claim.”

“What's your source for that?” “It is my own requirements document; the arithmetic and its monthly typo are printed on the page, my split into hours and decision risk is a model, and the accuracy, the parity gate and the four-percent catch are in my interview workbook.”

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — The program's weekly review needed seven operating metrics, and two of them — tickets generated and tickets per active container — required joining the support ticketing system to container data. The tickets did not carry a container ID. So the numbers were assembled each Monday by hand from four systems into spreadsheets: system accuracy 60%, one person able to produce them, and silent data loss nobody could see. A VP was steering the program by a number that only one person could produce.

T — Define the data contract and the acceptance test: one grain, a join that survives missing keys, definitions in code, a reconciliation gate to cut over, and a failure alert — and get the infrastructure team to build it by showing them the price of the status quo.

Baselines on the table: Four systems · 1 person · system accuracy 60% · 1.5–2 h per weekly snapshot (BRD) · 7 frozen metrics · tickets with no container ID · no failure alarm · priced baseline: BRD $129,600 a year → $103,680 difference (30 → 6 h/wk at an assumed $90/h), over $100K priced, not saved (my model: $100,360).

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery — documented the as-is — which systems, which pulls, which join — named the key gap (tickets without a container ID), froze seven metric definitions with their owners, and priced the status quo in the BRD (over $100K a year) so the infrastructure team could prioritize the build themselves. Exit bar: infra prioritizes the build off the price; scope is the seven metrics, nothing new.
  • Plan & Design — wrote the requirements (BR-1 to BR-4), chose the container × week grain, specified the documented ticket-to-container cross-reference, set BR-4 (feed-failure alert) and the stale-table alerts, and sketched the pipeline: scheduled extracts → S3 landing zone → SQS-triggered loads → Snowflake raw → curated metric tables → Looker SQL Runner views. Exit bar: requirements accepted by infra; acceptance gate published before build.
  • Build & Test — extract and load specs per system; the bridge table first; parity checks per metric; the parallel-run procedure, every mismatch explained by cause; the data-access security review that surfaced mid-build and cost two weeks. Exit bar: all seven metrics inside 1% of the manual pull for consecutive weeks; review closed.
  • Cutover & Go-Live — the cutover memo; the Monday deck switched to the pipeline; the manual pull kept running as rollback until parity had held a week beyond the gate. Exit bar: first Monday off the pipeline shows the same seven numbers.
  • Hypercare / Close — watched the failure alerts; triaged the first automated catch (a 4% ticket-sync drop) to a source cause; moved definitions into code; pushed the real container key upstream. Exit bar: accuracy 60% → 90%; the pipeline steers the review without me.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the as-is documentation, the grain choice, the bridge-table design, the seven frozen definitions, the pricing, the acceptance gate, the parallel-run procedure and the cutover memo. Not mine: the pipeline build and its alerts (infrastructure team), the real container key (Support), the security review (security team). I influenced by pricing the status quo in the BRD and making the cutover decision unarguable with a published gate.

Designed the process; others own it now. The definitions are in code, the gate is a documented procedure and the alerts page a team, not me. Trade-off put to leadership. New metrics for stability (the seven were frozen, with a parking lot), and two weeks of date for a data-access review I should have planned.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality (record vs modeled labeled)
  • $ — Over $100K a year, priced in the BRD, not saved: the BRD route gives $103,680 (30 → 6 h a week at an assumed $90/h); my model gives $100,360 ($30,160 hours + $70,200 decision risk). Both are priced, not claimed as savings. Realized candidate: 208 hours retired × $90 = $18,720 (modeled).
  • # — Four systems → one pipeline; seven definitions in code; no longer a single point of failure (one engineer maintains the pipeline instead of one PM rebuilding it, Apple A3); zero WBR deliveries skipped through the build (record).
  • % — System accuracy 60% → 90% (record); parity gate within 1% per metric for consecutive weeks (record); first automated catch a ticket-sync drop of about 4% (record).
  • Time — the weekly snapshot (1.5–2 h by hand per the BRD) → automated; the data-access review cost two weeks (record).
  • Customer — the VP and the core team steered on the same seven numbers the first Monday off the pipeline; Data Services and Support got tickets joined to containers for the first time.
  • Quality — every parallel-run mismatch explained by cause — mostly late-arriving tickets on the manual side; silence on a failed feed replaced by an alert.

Probing — five facets, then the full drill

Facet 1 — Data analysis (what data, grain, joins, cuts, what it revealed)

What I read. Three data sets named in the BRD record-from-docs: (1) Zendesk tickets — one row per ticket, created when the visibility service fails to start tracking a submitted container automatically, closed by Data Services; (2) Crux container data — pulled by hand into Google Sheets, keyed by container number; (3) Flexport core data in the warehouse. The unit of cost is a ticket; the unit of volume is a container; the ratio needs both, so I analyzed the grain at which each could be counted.

Data setUnit (grain)Key it carriesWhat it is used forWeakness found
Zendesk ticketsone row per ticketTicket ID; no container IDnumerator: tickets generatedcannot be joined to a container
Crux container dataone row per containerContainer Numberdenominator: active containers; coveragea manual pull into Google Sheets, taken once a week
Flexport core dataone row per shipment / containercontainer number, client, modecuts: 4PL vs 3PL; SLAnot joined to tickets until a bridge exists

The seven metrics the BRD froze, and the grain each lives at

#Metric (BRD wording)NumeratorDenominator / cutGrain
1Coverage — % of containers tracked (automated)containers tracked with no human inputactive containerscontainer-week stock
2Coverage — % of containers tracked (with human inputs)containers tracked after a human touchactive containerscontainer-week stock
3Cost to serve — tickets generatedtickets—ticket, counted per week
4Cost to serve — tickets generated / active containerticketsactive containersneeds the ticket → container join
5Timeliness — 4PL % within SLAcontainers inside SLA4PL containerscontainer-week, 4PL cut
6Timeliness — 3PL % within SLAcontainers inside SLA3PL containerscontainer-week, 3PL cut
7Timeliness — delta (4PL vs 3PL)metric 5 less metric 6—derived

Metric names are the BRD's. The numerator, denominator and grain columns are my design logic — the BRD does not define them. "Active container" is the review's own definition: cumulative inflow less containers 15 days past departed_terminal (Apple A2, grain 2).

What it revealed. (1) No key, no ratio: without a container ID on the ticket, tickets per active container could only be assembled by hand record-from-docs. (2) Grain decides the headline — in the real instrument: the review workbook (Apple A2, SV_28.xlsx) shows 80.6% automated at event grain (1,991 of 10,289 events human-touched = 19.4%), 54.1% at ticket grain (538 auto, 456 human) and 40.0% on 17 Jan falling to 21.9% on 31 Jan at weekly stock — three grains, three answers, so the grain label travels with every number (record). My worked join example makes the same point for the cost ratio: 12 tickets over 4 of 10 active containers give 18 rows at ticket grain, 12 / 18 = 0.667 instead of 12 / 10 = 1.20, a ratio 44.4% too good (illustrative — my design example, not program data). (3) Late-arriving tickets: the manual snapshot missed tickets that arrived after the pull — nearly every parallel-run mismatch was the manual side (record, Apple A3). (4) Silence was invisible: a ticket-sync drop of about 4% after a routine update looked like a quiet week in the manual era (record).

Risks named. The key gap, parity failure, one person as the single point of failure — and, as it turned out, the data-access review, which was not on the list. Lean / Kaizen mapping. The manual pull is motion and waiting waste; the seven frozen definitions are standard work; the parity gate is jidoka — stop the line when the pipeline and the reference disagree; the bridge table is poka-yoke for a missing key; "ship with the bridge, fix the source" is root-cause countermeasure at the right layer; every PDCA starts with the first alert.

Facet 5 — Probe questions (hardest follow-ups, numeric answers)

Why container × week?

Because the review reads weeks and the ratio needs numerator and denominator at one grain: tickets per active container. At ticket grain the container repeats once per ticket — my worked example gives 18 rows for 10 containers and a ratio of 0.667 instead of 1.20, 44.4% too good. At container-week every metric is a sum or a ratio of one table.

How did you join tickets that had no container ID?

A documented cross-reference table so the pipeline could ship, then a real join key pushed upstream so the fix outlived the workaround (record, Apple A3). The design I would add: count and list unmatched tickets, never silently drop them, with an unmatched-share alarm at 1% (threshold illustrative). I do not have a measured join rate to quote.

Why daily or hourly?

BR-3 says daily, preferably hourly; my whiteboard sketch is daily with hourly on the fast tables. The review is weekly, so daily is sufficient for the numbers; hourly matters for the failure alarm — a drop is caught the same day instead of the next.

What if parity had failed?

Then we would not have cut over: the manual pull stays the source of truth. Pass is within 1% on every frozen metric for consecutive weeks — 0.42% passes, 1.25% is investigate not fail (illustrative values), and in the cases I saw the explanation was late-arriving tickets on the manual side.

You wrote $103,680 in the BRD — what did you save?

Nothing I claim. It is a price: 3 × 5 × 2 × 4 × 12 × $90 (an assumed rate) = $129,600, less $25,920 after, $103,680 — and my own model prices it at $100,360 including decision risk. The realized candidate is the hours retired, 208 × $90 = $18,720, modeled, and I would measure it.

Why not wait for Support to add the container ID?

Because a program metric cannot wait on another team's roadmap; the bridge gave us the join now and the upstream fix made it cheap to retire the bridge. Ship with the bridge, fix the source.

What did the pipeline catch that you could not?

A ticket-sync drop of about 4% after a routine update (record, Apple A3). With my illustrative 1% alarm threshold that is 4× the threshold; in the manual era, silence read as a quiet week.

The hours term alone moves 4.30× across the three bases (129,600 / 30,160), before the decision-risk term. That spread is why the number is a price, never a saving. The decision-risk term ($70,200) has its own range, $12K–$117K.

Total impact statement — say it like this

The record number here is a price, not a saving. The manual way of producing the weekly review was priced in my requirements document at over a hundred thousand dollars a year — about a hundred and four thousand on its own arithmetic, thirty hours a week at an assumed ninety dollars an hour; my own model gets to about a hundred thousand with decision risk — and that price let the infrastructure team prioritize the build themselves. What I own is the design under it: a container-by-week grain so tickets and containers are counted together, a bridge table so the join could ship before the source was fixed, and a gate that said nothing switches until all seven metrics match the manual pull within one percent for consecutive weeks. System accuracy went from sixty to ninety percent, and the first automated catch was a ticket-sync drop of about four percent that nobody could have seen by hand. The realized version of the dollars is hours actually retired — about two hundred hours, roughly nineteen thousand at the requirements document's rate, modeled — and I won't call any of it savings.

If challenged — the full answers

"The requirements document says $103,680 saved. That's a lot for a spreadsheet."

It is a price, not a saving. The document's own arithmetic is 3 people × 5 hours × 2 reviews × $90, and its printed monthly figure does not match its annual. I say what the price was for — the BRD priced doing nothing so the infrastructure team could prioritize the build themselves — and I lead with system accuracy and the first catch, not dollars.

"Why build a pipeline for seven metrics?"

Because the ratio the program steered on — tickets per active container — had no join key and the number rested on one person stitching four systems by hand. Seven was the scope I held on purpose; new metrics went to a parking lot until the pipeline was stable.

"What's your actual technical contribution?"

The infrastructure team built the pipeline. Mine were the grain, the bridge-table design, the seven definitions, the pricing, the acceptance gate, the parallel-run procedure and the cutover memo — the part that decides whether a build is trusted.

20-second version and NACF bridge
20-second version

At Flexport the weekly review ran on four systems stitched by hand by one person. I wrote the requirements and the acceptance gate: a container-by-week grain, a cross-reference table for the missing ticket key, and a one-percent parity gate. System accuracy went from 60% to 90%, and the first automated catch was a ticket-sync drop of about 4%.

NACF bridge — one sentence

Capacity planning for inbound sits on the same data discipline: appointments, POs and containers must join at one grain (container × week or door × hour), unmatched records must be counted rather than dropped, and a feed that goes silent must raise an alarm — otherwise a quiet dock schedule looks like a healthy one.

Behavioral pack — four questions this story answers, each a separate 90-second STAR from a different facet

B1 · “Give me an example of a complex problem you solved with a simple solution. What made the problem complex? How do you know your solution addressed the problem?” AIQB: Invent and Simplify Q1 Invent and Simplify facet: solution design

Open (first two sentences): “The Monday review depended on four systems and one person assembling them by hand. I simplified it into one pipeline with seven frozen definitions and a bridge table for the one thing that was missing.”

90-second STAR. S Four systems, one person, accuracy 60%, tickets with no container ID. T Produce the same seven numbers without that person. A I wrote the requirements, chose a container-by-week grain, specified a ticket-to-container bridge so the join could ship before the source was fixed, froze seven definitions, and set a one-percent parity gate with the manual pull as rollback. R Accuracy 60% → 90%; four systems → one pipeline; the review no longer depends on me.

Land: four systems → one pipeline; 60% → 90%  ·  Pivot to NACF: a standard data contract for inbound: one grain, one bridge for missing keys, definitions in code.

Do NOT say: that I built the pipeline myself, or that the dollar is saved.

B2 · “Tell me about a situation that required you to dig deep to get to the root cause. How did you know you were focusing on the right things? What was the outcome? Would you have done anything differently?” AIQB: Dive Deep Q2 Dive Deep facet: data analysis

Open (first two sentences): “A join at ticket grain would have repeated each container once per ticket and made cost-to-serve look about forty-four percent better than it was. I caught that in the design, before it was in a deck.”

90-second STAR. S Cost-to-serve per active container was the program's headline ratio, and its join key did not exist. T Make the ratio right before anyone steered on it. A I laid out the grain of each data set, showed with a worked example that 12 tickets over 4 of 10 containers gives 18 rows and a ratio of 0.667 instead of 1.20, and moved the model to container × week with numerator and denominator at the same grain. The parallel run then showed most mismatches were late-arriving tickets on the manual side. R A ratio that foots, and a first automated catch — a 4% ticket-sync drop — that the manual era could not have seen.

Land: 0.667 vs 1.20 (illustrative); 4% drop (record)  ·  Pivot to NACF: any network ratio — utilization, cost per unit — is only as good as the grain of its numerator and denominator.

Do NOT say: that the 44.4% was a real finding in production — it is my design example; or that the manual numbers were never right.

B3 · “Tell me about a time when you generated a creative solution to a problem or project without requiring additional resources. What was the problem? What was the solution and how did you come up with it? What was the outcome?” AIQB: Frugality Q5 Frugality facet: implementation

Open (first two sentences): “I didn't ask for budget. I priced the manual process in the BRD, so the infrastructure team put the build on their own roadmap, and I held the scope to seven metrics.”

90-second STAR. S The pipeline belonged to another organization with its own priorities. T Get it built without owning or funding it. A I priced the status quo in the BRD — its labor arithmetic gave $103,680 and my model $100,360 including decision risk; I held scope at seven metrics with a parking lot and used the existing BI tool's SQL runner. R Infrastructure prioritized the build itself; no new system bought; zero review deliveries skipped through the build.

Land: over $100K priced, not saved  ·  Pivot to NACF: a business case for dock or yard automation: price the status quo, scope tight, no new tool unless the numbers demand it.

Do NOT say: the $103,680 as savings; or the hours basis as settled — it is the BRD's 30 hours a week at an assumed $90 an hour, not my ~4 hours.

B4 · “Describe a time when you refused to compromise your standards around quality/customer service, etc. Who was your customer? What was the result?” AIQB: Highest Standards Q1 Insist on the Highest Standards facet: probe questions

Open (first two sentences): “Nothing switched until all seven metrics matched the manual pull within one percent for consecutive weeks, with every mismatch explained by cause, and a failed feed raising an alert.”

90-second STAR. S A VP steered the program by this number, and the pipeline was new. T Earn the cutover instead of announcing it. A I published the acceptance gate before build; ran the parallel run; investigated every mismatch — 1.25% is investigate, not fail (my illustrative example); kept the manual pull as rollback a week beyond parity; and required the failure alert (BR-4) before go-live. R The first Monday off the pipeline showed the same seven numbers, and silence stopped meaning health.

Land: 1% gate; 0.42% pass (illustrative)  ·  Pivot to NACF: cutting over a yard-management or appointment system: parallel run to a numeric gate, rollback kept, alarm on silence.

Do NOT say: that the 1% threshold on the alarm was in the record — it is illustrative; or the number of consecutive weeks.

Same story, three angles — three cards, three openers, three metrics, three LPs

Data / analytical angle Dive Deep

Opener: “A ticket-grain join would have made cost-to-serve look forty-four percent better than reality — so before we built anything, I fixed the grain.”

Headline metric: 12 / 18 = 0.667 vs 12 / 10 = 1.20 (illustrative); 4% drop (record)

  • Unit of cost (ticket) vs unit of volume (container); one grain: container × week.
  • Unmatched tickets counted and listed, never dropped.
  • Parity: 0.42% pass; 1.25% investigate (illustrative); mismatches the manual side's late tickets.
  • Say "illustrative" on every example number.

Conflict / influence / backbone angle Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit

Opener: “Everyone wanted one more metric during the build; I held the scope at seven with a parking lot, and shipped the bridge instead of waiting for Support.”

Headline metric: seven frozen metrics; one bridge table

  • Stability over coverage — a documented trade-off, not an oversight.
  • Bridge now, real key upstream: another team's roadmap did not hold the program.
  • Priced in the BRD so infra could prioritize it themselves — influence without authority.
  • My seat (Apple A3): the BRD, the seven definitions, the parallel-run gate, the acceptance test, the cutover call; infra built the pipeline.

Honest failure-and-learning angle Earn Trust

Opener: “I discovered the data-access security review mid-build instead of planning it — two weeks — on the pipeline that was supposed to retire my own manual review.”

Headline metric: two-week security-review miss; zero Monday reviews skipped

  • I owned it upward immediately: the slip was scoping, not engineering — the BRD priced the build and never the review (Apple G3).
  • Ticket data carries customer content, so the access review is a gate with lead time, not a formality; I had treated it as a formality.
  • I re-sequenced instead of stalling: the parallel run kept the Monday review alive on the manual path, so no review week was skipped.
  • I closed the review properly — cross-reference table documented, data classes named, who could read what — and now every data program starts with "whose approval does this data need, and how long does that take?"
  • Not a dollar claim: the miss cost margin, not delivery. Do not mix in the $103,680 here.
Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups

"Your document says $103,680 saved. So you saved over a hundred thousand?"

Numeric answer. No. 3 × 5 × 2 × 4 × 12 × $90 (an assumed rate) = $129,600; after = $25,920; difference $103,680 — a priced labor estimate, and the printed monthly figure ($10,880) does not match its annual ($10,800 × 12). My model prices the baseline at $100,360 ($30,160 + $70,200). The realized candidate is the hours actually retired: 208 × $90 = $18,720, modeled.

Say exactly: “It's a price, not a saving — about a hundred and four thousand on the document's labor basis, about a hundred thousand in my model. The realized version is hours retired, about nineteen thousand, modeled.”

"Pipeline 405 tickets, manual 400. That's a 1.25% gap — a failure?"

Numeric answer. Investigate, not fail (illustrative values): |405 − 400| / 400 = 1.25%, just over the 1% gate, and the explanation in the cases I saw was five late-arriving tickets on the manual side. Contrast 70.9% vs 71.2% coverage: 0.42%, pass. (Example values are illustrative.)

Say exactly: “A gap of one-point-two-five percent is over the gate, so we investigate — and the cause was late tickets the manual pull missed. Coverage at point-four-two percent passed.”

"How do you know the 4% drop wasn't noise?"

Numeric answer. The alarm rule is 1 − sync rate > 1% (threshold illustrative); the observed 1 − 0.96 = 4% is 4× that threshold, and the record says it was a ticket-sync drop of about 4% after a routine update, invisible in the manual era; I did not keep the triage detail beyond that. A threshold like this is tuned from weeks of normal variation, which I would pull from the history.

Say exactly: “Four percent is four times the alarm threshold, and in the manual era it would have looked like a quiet week. I triaged it to a source cause, not to noise.”

Backup bench — use only if a primary doesn't fit

Three fallbacks for the hiring manager, none of them another interviewer's primary: S9 (Elementum — the 597-lane Pareto and the ≥95% gate, for Backbone / Think Big / Highest Standards if S11 and S12 are spent), S19 (Elementum · Walmart-client — the promise buffer as one governed dial; a design-flavored Frugality / Invent & Simplify alternate, all model, illustrative labels intact) and S13 (Manhattan — Cyber Monday at 4 AM, the Bias for Action / Customer Obsession alternate). Tell S11 and S12 first.

Bench discipline. The two (or four) primaries above are the stories to tell first. Reach for a bench story only when the question genuinely does not fit a primary, or when the interviewer asks for "another example" on an LP already spent. Each entry is a 60-second pitch, a compressed STAR, one results line and two probes — enough to answer and stop; no full probing bank or derivation. Where a bench story is another interviewer's primary, the red note says so: use it only if the question demands it, and keep the facts identical to that packet.

Backup — only if askedS9 — Elementum — 1,000 supplier/carrier routes asked, 597 built first: X12 EDI onboarding behind a ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate

LPs this backup can serve: Have Backbone; Disagree & Commit (primary — 597 vs 1,000, two levels up) · Deliver Results · Insist on the Highest Standards (the gate held twice) · Think Big (the staged 1,000-lane vision) · Broad Responsibility (small carriers kept inside the standard by the portal floor)
Basis. I owned the scope analysis and the onboarding standard, influencing an executive two levels above my sponsor; engineers built the maps and harness to the standard; Transportation owned the carrier relationships. Cohort sizes and the number of cohorts are not in the record — do not quote them.
60-second pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. The Global Head of Supply Chain wanted inbound visibility on 1,000 lanes in two months; shipment-data completeness was 60% and high-value lanes were blind. Task. I owned the scope analysis and the onboarding standard, influencing an executive two levels above my sponsor. Action. Discovery: a spend Pareto — 597 lanes carried 80% of freight spend — pre-validated with Transportation and Procurement before I took it up. Plan & Design: the onboarding standard (X12 850/855/856/940/945/214/997 with an XML/portal floor — "the standard was the data, not the transport") and a cohort gate of ≥95% valid ASN. Build & Test: a mapping-and-test harness and a scorecard per lane. Cutover: 597 live in month one; one cohort failed the gate twice on a timestamp format and I held it both times. Hypercare: completeness scorecards to 95%; ~200 lanes the next wave behind the same gate. Lesson: automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers — the gate is the product. Result. Primary metric: inbound shipment-data completeness 60% → 95%. Freight spend −10% ≈ $2M in six months (record); 597 lanes = 80% of spend live in month one; adoption 8×.

Compressed STAR
  • S — 1,000 global lanes asked in two months — scope, schedule and quality all fixed; completeness 60%; lanes differed by orders of magnitude in spend and carriers differed in EDI capability.
  • T — Own the scope analysis and the onboarding standard; take the re-scope two levels up with the evidence attached.
  • A — Spend Pareto (597 = 80% of spend) pre-validated so no lane owner could contradict it in the room; standard + ≥95% valid-ASN gate published to carriers before cohort one; harness so later cohorts were mostly configuration; held a failing cohort twice; ~400 lanes carrying ~20% of spend deferred — priced in spend share, not lane count.
  • R — Completeness 60 → 95%; 100% visibility on high-value lanes; 597 lanes live month one; freight −10% ≈ $2M in six months (record); method written up as the sourcing SOP.
Results line

Completeness 60% → 95% (record) · 597 of 1,000 lanes = 80% of freight spend live in month one (record) · freight spend −10% ≈ $2M in six months (record — kept as measured, never annualized) · adoption 8× (record) · ≈$0.4M/yr document-handling labor (modeled add-on).

Two probes

You defied a Global Head of Supply Chain's number. How?

With the spend curve and the lane owners' validation attached — 597 lanes carried 80% of the spend, so the phased plan was argued in spend share, not lane count. He accepted the phasing off the curve; I then committed to the remainder behind the same gate and delivered it.

Is the $2M annualized?

No — it is the record as measured: minus ten percent on the onboarded lanes' spend in six months. My three-way split (routing-guide compliance ~$0.8M, mode discipline ~$0.6M, expedites and detention ~$0.6M) decomposes the record; it never adds to it.

Canon numbers (basis labels): 60% → 95% completeness (record) · 597 / 1,000 lanes = 80% of spend (record) · −10% ≈ $2M in 6 months (record) · ≥95% valid-ASN gate, held twice (record) · 8× adoption (record) · ≈$0.4M/yr labor (modeled). Never: cohort counts or sizes; never double the $2M into a run-rate.

Backup — only if askedS19 — Elementum (client: Walmart Marketplace) — the delivery-promise buffer rebuilt as priced risk: one governed dial, +32 points of fast-promise share at the same 95% floor (all model, no record dollar)

LPs this backup can serve: Dive Deep (alternate when S8/S10 are spent) · Invent and Simplify (alternate) · Frugality (speed bought with zero new spend) — design-flavored probes
Basis. Engagement lead for the promise-policy workstream; the client's promise-engineering and data-science teams owned and built the engine and the transit models — I never configured or deployed any platform. Every quantity is from the engagement's illustrative twelve-cohort model — say "the model" in the same breath; there is no production dollar and none is ever promoted. Labels (metro two-day, mountain rural) are the model's illustrative cohort names.
60-second pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. A marketplace client printing delivery dates on inventory it never touches: promised date = order time + seller handling + predicted transit + a safety buffer — and the buffer was a flat 1.10 days on every lane regardless of variability. In the engagement model — twelve lane cohorts, about a million orders a week, transit σ from 0.22 to 1.60 days — that flat buffer printed 95.8% accuracy but put only 7.6% of orders on a fast promise: dense metro lanes over-insured and fat-tailed rural lanes under-insured at the same time. Task. Own the decomposition, the alternatives, the validation gates and the governance recommendation; the client's data science and engineering did not report to me. Action. Discovery: the buffer-waste audit — the flat buffer bought Φ(5.0), near-certainty, on the tightest cohort and only Φ(0.69) ≈ 75% on the widest. Plan & Design: four policies on one comparison table — uniform, σ-proportional with one governed dial, minimum-days at the floor, profit-optimal on priced breach economics. Build & Test: b = 1.645σ holds 95% on every cohort by construction; the equimarginal check proved the optimum; the Q4 drill showed a frozen summer dial breaking the floor. Cutover: four shadow weeks against logged live orders before any displayed promise. Hypercare: the dial published in the weekly review header, re-solved before peak. Result. Primary metric: fast-promise share 7.6% → 39.6%, +32 points at the held 95% floor; average promised day 3.09 → 2.75; +$255K a week, about $13.3M a year — all on the illustrative book, and I say "the model" in the same breath.

Compressed STAR
  • S — A flat 1.10-day buffer on every lane: 95.8% headline accuracy hiding over-insurance on tight lanes and under-insurance on loose ones; fast-promise share 7.6% (model).
  • T — Lead the promise-policy workstream: decompose the waste, put priced alternatives on one table, design the gates, leave a governance instrument — not a tuning habit.
  • A — Promise identity and accuracy factorization; buffer-waste audit; four policies on one table; proofs (Φ(1.645) = 0.95 per cohort, equimarginal check, Q4 drill at ~1.45× σ); shadow mode for four weeks; the dial k owned by a business owner and printed every Monday.
  • R — Fast-promise share 7.6 → 39.6% (+32 pts) at exactly the 95% floor; average promised day 3.09 → 2.75; +$255K/wk ≈ $13.3M/yr — model, labeled; the widest cohorts got honestly slower promises (1.10 → 2.63 days) on purpose.
Results line

Fast-promise share 7.6% → 39.6% (+32.0 points) at a held 95.0% floor (model) · average promised day 3.09 → 2.75 (model) · +$255K/wk ≈ $13.3M/yr vs uniform (modeled on the illustrative ~1M-order book) · the profit-optimal frontier (97.8% accuracy, ≈ +$20M/yr) handed to Finance as a priced decision — shown, never claimed, never merged with the $13.3M.

Two probes

The whole story rests on an illustrative cohort set.

It does, deliberately — the model exists so the argument happens in numbers instead of adjectives. If the real σ looks nothing like the model's, k changes and the lane ranking changes; what survives any parameter set is the structure: buffers risk-proportional, the last buffer-day buying the same accuracy everywhere, and a floor that disagrees with the breach economics treated as a governance finding.

Why one dial instead of per-lane learned buffers?

Data science wanted per-lane learned buffers — more model, more value, invisible governance. I priced the difference and recommended the one-dial policy first: it captures most of the value, it is auditable, and σ improvements flow into speed automatically and visibly. The no went into the comparison table as a priced row, not a meeting minute.

Canon numbers (basis labels): 7.6% → 39.6% fast-promise share (+32 pts, model) · 95.8% → 95.0% accuracy held at the floor (model) · 3.09 → 2.75 promised days (model) · +$255K/wk ≈ $13.3M/yr (modeled) · 12 cohorts, ~1M orders/wk, σ 0.22–1.60 days, k = 1.645 (illustrative model) · breach ≈$12 vs margin ≈$6.50 (model assumptions). Never a production dollar.

Backup — only if askedS13 — Manhattan — Cyber Monday, 4 AM: allocation across 3 DCs and 100 stores when the scheduler died

LPs this backup can serve: Bias for Action (primary) · Customer Obsession (primary backup — the promise ranked first) · Deliver Results
Basis. OMS consultant on allocation, promising and the OMS–WMS handshake; engineering fixed the scheduler once I isolated it; the post-mortem mechanisms were mine to drive. $8M / $6M / 99% are record; the margin figures are my model at benchmark rates. Older story — if asked for recent, pair with the UDC robotics throttle (S2): same muscle, 2024–25.
60-second pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Manhattan OMS consultant on allocation and promising across 3 DCs and 100 stores; 4 AM Cyber Monday the VP called — backorders piling with inventory present, ~$6M of sales at risk against a 95% fill-rate goal. Task. Own the response before the root cause was known. Action. Discovery: the contradiction (ATP > 0, backorders rising) was the entry bar to declare an incident. Plan & Design: I ranked by promise-at-risk, not FIFO; node choice = the promise — near 0.85 days vs far 4.60. Build & Test: one bridge, every rebalance timestamped; reason-coded alerts I had already built. Cutover: rebalanced so no DC drowned; status every two hours until 9 PM. Hypercare: root cause — the allocation scheduler died silently at the daylight-saving change; the post-mortem shipped a heartbeat, a DST checklist, a 3σ page rule and a rehearsed rebalance playbook. Lesson: decide reversibly, log it, find the cause later. Result. Primary metric: peak fill rate — 95% goal → 99% delivered. ~$8M of peak sales protected against ~$6M at risk (record); my model puts that at ≈$3.2M of gross margin at a 40% benchmark margin; structural stockouts −20% over the engagement.

Compressed STAR
  • S — 4 AM Cyber Monday: backorder lines piling with inventory present; promises aging; ~$6M at risk against a 95% fill goal; nobody had written a clearing rule for failure.
  • T — Own the response before the cause was known, without making it worse.
  • A — Declared the incident on the contradiction; clearing rule — promise-at-risk, not FIFO — written on the bridge log in the first hour so nobody re-argued it at noon; rebalanced across three DCs; two-hourly status; isolated the DST scheduler death through my own reason-coded alerts; blameless post-mortem → heartbeat, DST checklist, 3σ page rule, rehearsed playbook.
  • R — Fill 99% vs the 95% goal; ~$8M protected vs ~$6M at risk (record pair); structural stockouts −20% over the engagement; the next peak ran boring.
Results line

Peak fill 99% vs the 95% goal (record) · ~$8M of peak sales protected vs ~$6M at risk (record pair — one-time sales that didn't die, never a saving) · ≈$3.2M gross margin at a 40% benchmark (modeled) · structural stockouts −20% over the engagement (record) · detection moved from a VP phone call to one heartbeat interval.

Two probes

Why act before you knew the cause?

Because waiting had an hourly price — the queue was aging while inventory sat idle — and a reversible, logged intervention did not. I checked with engineering first that manual clears could not corrupt allocation, timestamped every rebalance, and hunted the cause in parallel with alerts I had already built.

Isn't this a hero story?

The event is; the post-mortem is the point. Carrying the first stretch solo is one of my do-differentlys. What I would present as the result is the mechanism set — heartbeat, DST checklist, 3σ page rule, rehearsed playbook — and that the next peak ran boring.

Canon numbers (basis labels): 99% fill vs 95% goal (record) · ~$8M protected vs ~$6M at risk (record) · 3 DCs / 100 stores (record) · near 0.85 d vs far 4.60 d (record) · −20% structural stockouts (record) · ≈$3.2M margin at 40% (modeled). Never "$2MM baseline"; never present $8M as a saving.
